\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\mathscr{F})^{tK_{U}t^{-1}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}.

The map
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})
induces a

T^{+}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-equivariant map
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}
which is
a quasi-isomorphism on the finite slope part. Indeed, it is easy to see that we have factorizations:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}t^{-1}}(V_{0}t^{-1},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}t^{-1}}(V_{0}t^{-1},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

On the finite slope part, the maps
t
are quasi-isomorphisms, therefore the maps
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}t^{-1}}(V_{0}t^{-1},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U},f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U},f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
are also quasi-isomorphisms. Now we can take
t\in T^{++}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
. Since
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}=\operatorname{colim}_{n}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}t^{n}}(V_{0}t^{-n},\mathscr{F})^{K_{U},f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
, the colimit is constant and we conclude.

We now consider compact open subgroups
K_{p,n}
of
G({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
which
admits an Iwahori decomposition
K_{p,n}=K_{U}\times K_{\bar{B},n}

where
K_{\bar{B},n}
is the principal level
p^{n}
congruence
subgroup in
\bar{B}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
. We let
K^{\prime}_{p,n}
be the compact open
subgroup of
G({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
which admit an Iwahori decomposition
K^{\prime}_{p,n}=K_{U}\times T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\times K_{\bar{U},n}
where
K_{\bar{U},n}
is the principal level
p^{n}
congruence subgroup in
\bar{U}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
. Note that
K_{p,n}\subseteq K^{\prime}_{p,n}
is a normal subgroup and that
K^{\prime}_{p,n}/K_{p,n}=T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
.

We have that
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0}}(V_{0},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{K_{U}}=\operatorname{colim}_{m,n}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K_{p,n}})
. Moreover,

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K_{p,n}})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}=\oplus_{\chi:T({\mathbf{Z}}/p^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi)).

We claim that each
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))
can be equipped with an action of
T^{+}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
and on the finite slope quotient the maps:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))

for
n^{\prime}\geq n
are quasi-isomorphisms, and the maps:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))

for
m^{\prime}\geq m
are quasi-isomorphism.
This follows from the property that for
t\in T^{++}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
sufficiently regular, we have factorizations:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}}(\chi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}}(\chi))}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

as well as factorizations:

\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n^{\prime}}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{t}
\textstyle{\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m,K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

We conclude, since by definition (see just after Theorem 5.4.14
in
[
BP21
]
) we have
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{F_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}}}(V_{0,m^{\prime},K^{\prime}_{p,n}},\omega^{\kappa}_{K^{\prime}_{p,n}}(\chi))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\kappa,\chi)^{+,f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
.
∎

Let
\nu:T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}_{p}^{\times}
be an analytic character.
[
BP21
, Sect. 5]
We have cohomology theories
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w,an}(K^{p},\nu)^{+,f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w,an}(K^{p},\nu,\mathrm{cusp})^{+,f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
, defined in

[
BP21
, Sect. 6.4]
(see just after Theorem
6.4.10
there). Let
d\nu\in X^{*}(T)_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
be the differential of
\nu
. Define
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
by the formula

d\nu=-w^{-1}w_{0,M}(\kappa+\rho)-\rho
.

Theorem 4.6.57
.

We have canonical isomorphisms of
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-modules (where the decomposition on the right hand side corresponds to the decomposition into isotypic parts for the action of
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
):

\displaystyle HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{\mathrm{cusp},d\nu,w}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{-w^{-1}w_{0,M}\kappa})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oplus_{\nu^{\prime}:T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}_{p}^{\times},~d\nu^{\prime}=d\nu}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\nu^{\prime},\mathrm{cusp})^{+,f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}

\displaystyle HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{d\nu,w}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{-w^{-1}w_{0,M}\kappa})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\oplus_{\nu^{\prime}:T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}_{p}^{\times},~d\nu^{\prime}=d\nu}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(K^{p},\nu^{\prime})^{+,f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}

Proof.

This is similar to the proof of Theorem
4.6.56
and left to the reader.
∎

We have the following theorem which slightly generalizes
[
BP21
, Thm. 1.2.1, Thm. 6.7.3]
.

Theorem 4.6.58
.

The functor
HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{w,\lambda}
has
cohomological amplitude
[\ell(w),d]
, and
HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{\mathrm{cusp},w,\lambda}
has cohomological
amplitude
[0,\ell(w)]
.

Proof.

Given Theorem
4.6.45
, we simply need to see
that the functor
(-)^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
is exact on higher Coleman theories. But one sees
(see Theorem
4.6.56
and its proof) that the
finite slope part is obtained by taking the finite slope part of an
inductive system of complexes of Banach spaces acted on by a compact operator,
and
(-)^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
is exact in this case, see
[
BP21
, Proposition
5.1.4]
. We remark that we could also deduce this theorem
directly from
[
BP21
, Thm. 1.2.1, Thm. 6.7.3]
(which is the
current theorem in the case of
HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{w,\lambda}
and
HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{\mathrm{cusp},w,\lambda}
applied to Vermas) by using a diagram chase similar to the
proof of Theorem
2.3.32
.∎

4.6.59.
Bounds on slopes for higher Coleman theory

Using
[
BP21
]
and
[
BP23
]
, we can obtain bounds for the slopes.

Theorem 4.6.60
.

Assume that either the Shimura variety is proper or that we are in the Siegel case. Let
w\in{}^{M}W
. Let
M\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{m}_{w},\mathfrak{b}_{M_{w}})_{\lambda-alg}
be a module generated by a highest weight vector of weight
\nu
.
Then the slopes of
HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{\mathrm{cusp},w,\lambda}(M)
and
HC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{w,\lambda}(M)
are
\geq\lambda-\nu+w^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho+\rho
.

Remark 4.6.61
.

The finite slope projector and the notion of slope
\geq\lambda-\nu+w^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho+\rho
were explained in section
4.6.46
.

Remark 4.6.62
.

We conjecture (
[
BP21
, Conj. 6.8.1]
) that the theorem should hold for any Shimura variety. The slightly weaker bound
\geq\lambda-\nu
is currently available in full generality by
[
BP21
, Thm. 6.8.3]
.

Proof of Theorem
4.6.60
.

For
M=M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{\nu}
a Verma module with highest
weight
\nu
, this follows from
[
BP21
, Thm. 6.10.1]
for the proper case, and

[
BP23
, Cor. 6.2.16]
in the Siegel case, together with
Theorem
4.6.57
(the paper
[
BP23
]

shows that the strongly small slope condition which is sometimes needed in

[
BP21
]
can be weakened to the small slope condition in the
symplectic case). Since any
module
M
as in the statement of the theorem admits a resolution by Verma
modules
M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{\nu^{\prime}}
with
\nu\geq\nu^{\prime}
, we are done. ∎

4.7.
p
-adic Eichler Shimura theory

Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)_{E}
. We define functors:

\displaystyle CC_{\lambda}:{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{b})_{\lambda-alg}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle D(\Mod^{\lambda-\mathrm{sm}}_{B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})}(E))

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})

\displaystyle CC_{\mathrm{cusp},\lambda}:{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{b})_{\lambda-alg}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle D(\Mod^{\lambda-\mathrm{sm}}_{B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})}(E))

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M,\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}}).

Theorem 4.7.1
.

For any
M\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{b})_{\lambda-alg}
, we have

CC_{\lambda}(M)\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},VB^{\operatorname{red}}(\operatorname{Loc}(M)))

and

CC_{\mathrm{cusp},\lambda}(M)\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},VB^{\operatorname{red}}(\operatorname{Loc}(M))\otimes_{\mathscr{O}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}}\mathscr{I}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})

Moreover, the action of
\mu\in Z(\mathfrak{m})
on
\operatorname{Loc}(M)
via the
horizontal action induces an arithmetic Sen operator on the left hand side.

Proof.

By Theorem
4.4.1
, we have
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}}{\otimes}_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{\textrm{an}}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})
.
We consider the category
\Mod^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})
of sheaves of
solid
\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}
-modules equipped with an action
of
\mathfrak{g}
, and the similarly-defined category

\Mod^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathbf{C}}_{p})
.. These are abelian categories, with enough
injectives. We consider the diagram of functors:

\textstyle{\Mod^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Mod(\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{sm}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Mod^{\prime}_{\mathfrak{g}}({\mathbf{C}}_{p})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\Mod({\mathbf{C}}_{p})}

where the horizontal arrows are given by taking
\mathfrak{g}
-invariants and the vertical arrows are given by taking global sections.
This diagram is
2
-commutative, and it induces a
2
-commutative diagram at the level of bounded derived categories.
We deduce that
CC_{\lambda}(M)\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}))
. We therefore need to show that
VB^{\operatorname{red}}(\operatorname{Loc}(M))=\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})
. To this end, the Chevalley–Eilenberg resolution
yields

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})=[M^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\rightarrow M^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\otimes\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\rightarrow\cdots\rightarrow M^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\otimes\Lambda^{r}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}]

in degrees
0
up to
r=\mathrm{dim}\mathfrak{g}
.
By Theorem
4.5.20
(4) (and the flatness of
M^{\vee}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}
), we have

VB^{0}(M^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{C}^{\mathrm{la}}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee})=M^{\vee}\otimes\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\otimes\Lambda^{i}\mathfrak{g}^{\vee},

and we deduce (see Remark
4.5.18
) that

VB^{\operatorname{red}}(\operatorname{Loc}(M))=\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(M,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}})
. The cuspidal case is identical.
The final claim regarding the Sen operator follows from Theorem
4.5.23
.
∎

Theorem 4.7.2
.

Assume that
\lambda
is non-Liouville.
For any
M\in{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{g},\mathfrak{b})_{\lambda-\mathrm{alg}}
, we have that
H^{i}(VB^{\operatorname{red}}(\operatorname{Loc}(M)))=VB^{0}(H^{i}(\operatorname{Loc}(M))
.
We have a spectral sequence:

E_{1}^{p,q}=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell(w)=p}H^{p+q}(HC_{w,\lambda}(E\otimes^{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{P_{w}}}M))

converging to

H^{p+q}(CC_{\lambda}(M))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}
.
Similarly, we have a spectral sequence:

E_{1}^{p,q}=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell(w)=p}H^{p+q}(HC_{\mathrm{cusp},w,\lambda}(E\otimes^{L}_{\mathfrak{u}_{P_{w}}}M))

converging to

H^{p+q}(CC_{\mathrm{cusp},\lambda}(M))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}
.
In all cases
w\mu\in Z(\mathfrak{m}_{w})
acting on

H_{*}(\mathfrak{u}_{P_{w}},M)
induces an arithmetic Sen operator.

Proof.

To prove the first part, it suffices to show that the cohomology
sheaves
H^{i}(\operatorname{Loc}(M))
is acyclic for the functor
VB^{\operatorname{red}}
. We can check
this acyclicity locally, and in particular after restricting to Bruhat strata,
where it follows from Theorems
3.5.11
and
4.5.20
(5).
Finally, the spectral sequence is then a simple consequence of
Proposition
4.6.13
together with Theorems
4.7.1
and
3.5.11
.
∎

Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)_{E}
be a non-Liouville weight. We apply the above
theorem to the Verma module
M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}
, giving the following
results.

Corollary 4.7.3
.

Assume that
\lambda
is antidominant in the sense of
Remark
3.5.21
, and that the Shimura variety is proper. Then
CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})
is concentrated in the middle degree
d
and moreover, it has a decreasing filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))
with

•

\mathrm{Fil}^{d+1}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))=0
,

•

\mathrm{Fil}^{0}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))=\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))
,

•

\mathrm{Gr}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell(w)=p}H^{p}(HC_{w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{\lambda+w^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho+\rho}))
.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
4.7.2
, because the
spectral sequence degenerates by a combination of Corollary
3.5.20

(noting Remark
3.5.21
) and Theorem
4.6.45

(noting that since the Shimura variety is proper,
HC_{\mathrm{cusp},w,\lambda}=HC_{w,\lambda}
).
∎

Remark 4.7.4
.

We see that in the antidominant case, the highest weights appearing in the
p
-adic Eichler–Shimura decomposition follow the exact same pattern as the highest weights appearing in the classical Eichler–Shimura theory.

We now consider the general case where
\lambda
need not be antidominant, and we take the “ordinary” part.

Theorem 4.7.5
.

Assume that we are in the Siegel case or that
the Shimura variety is proper. Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)_{E}
be a non-Liouville weight. We have that

CC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})
and
CC^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}_{\mathrm{cusp},\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})
have slope
\geq 0
. Moreover, we have a
spectral sequence:

E_{1}^{p,q}=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell(w)=p}H^{2p+q-d}(HC^{=0}_{w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{\lambda+w^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho+\rho}))

converging to

H^{p+q}(CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}
,
and similarly for cuspidal cohomology.

Proof.

This is a combination of Theorem
4.7.2
, Corollary
3.5.20
and Theorem
4.6.60
.
∎

Finally we have the following corollary.

Corollary 4.7.6
.

Assume that the Shimura variety is
proper. Let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)_{E}
be a non-Liouville weight. Then
CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))
is concentrated in the middle degree
d
and moreover, it has a decreasing filtration
\mathrm{Fil}^{i}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))
with

•

\mathrm{Fil}^{d+1}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))=0
,

•

\mathrm{Fil}^{0}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))=\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))
,

•

\mathrm{Gr}^{p}\mathrm{H}^{d}(CC^{=0}_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda}))=\oplus_{w\in{}^{M}W,\ell(w)=p}H^{p}(HC^{=0}_{w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{w})_{\lambda+w^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho+\rho}))
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of Theorem
4.7.5
and Theorem
4.6.57
.
∎

Remark 4.7.7
.

Thus, we see that on the ordinary part the highest weights appearing in the
p
-adic Eichler–Shimura decomposition follow the same pattern as the highest weights appearing in the classical Eichler–Shimura theory.

4.8.
The classical Hodge–Tate decomposition for

\mathrm{GSp}_{4}

We now specialize the theory to the group
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
and first review the
classical Hodge–Tate decomposition. From now on we use the notation for

\GSp_{4}
introduced in Section
1.8.8
.
Let
\kappa=(k_{1},k_{2};w)\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
be a dominant weight for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
. The dominance condition is
0\geq k_{1}\geq k_{2}
. We let
V_{\kappa}
be the corresponding highest weight representation. We let
\mathcal{V}_{\kappa,K_{p}}^{\vee}
be the pro-Kummer étale local system on
\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}}
, attached to
V_{\kappa}^{\vee}
.

The coherent weights appearing in the Hodge–Tate decomposition of the local system
\mathcal{V}_{\kappa,K_{p}}^{\vee}
are the

\{-w_{0,M}(w.\kappa+2\rho^{M})\}
where
w\in{{}^{M}W}
(see
[
FC90
]
, Thm. 6.2).
We make this explicit: the set
\{-w_{0,M}(w.\kappa+2\rho^{M})\}
consists exactly of
(3-k_{2},3-k_{1};-w),(3-k_{2},k_{1}+1;-w),(2-k_{1},k_{2};-w),(k_{1},k_{2};-w)
.
We recall that our conventions are that the cyclotomic character
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}(1)
has
Hodge–Tate weight
-1
and that the Sen operator acts via
1
on the Sen module
of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}(1)
, so that the (generalized) Hodge–Tate weights are the negatives
of the eigenvalues of the Sen operator.
Given our choice of parabolic
P_{\mu}
, we have
\mu=(-1/2,-1/2;1/2)\in X_{*}(T)_{E}
. By Theorem
4.5.20
,
\mu
is an arithmetic Sen operator.

Remark 4.8.1

(reality check)
.

This is consistent with the fact that the tautological exact sequence over
\mathcal{FL}
is

0\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{(0,-1;1)}\rightarrow St\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{FL}}\rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{(1,0;1)}\rightarrow 0

which pulls back to

0\rightarrow Lie(A)_{K_{p}}(1)\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\rightarrow T_{p}A\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\rightarrow(\omega_{A^{t}})_{K_{p}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\rightarrow 0.

We see that
Lie(A)
has Sen weight

1=\langle(0,-1;1),(-1/2,-1/2;1/2)\rangle

(and Hodge–Tate weight
-1
), while
w_{A^{t}}
has weight
0=\langle(1,0;1),(-1/2,-1/2;1/2)\rangle
.

The Hodge–Tate weight attached to the sheaf
\omega^{(l_{1},l_{2};w)}
is
\frac{l_{1}+l_{2}-w}{2}
. Thus, in the Hodge–Tate decomposition of the cohomology of
V_{\kappa}^{\vee}
, the Hodge–Tate weights are given by the formula:

(k_{1},k_{2};-w)\mapsto\frac{k_{1}+k_{2}+w}{2}
,
(2-k_{1},k_{2};-w)\mapsto\frac{2-k_{1}+k_{2}+w}{2}
,
(3-k_{2},k_{1}+1;-w)\mapsto\frac{4-k_{2}+k_{1}+w}{2}
,
(3-k_{2},3-k_{1};-w)\mapsto\frac{6-k_{1}-k_{2}+w}{2}
.

Theorem 4.8.2

(
[
FC90
]
, Thm. 6.2)
.

We have the following
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\times\mathbf{T}_{K_{p}K^{p}}
-equivariant isomorphisms:

\mathrm{H}^{i}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{\kappa,K_{p}})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=

H^{i}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(k_{1},k_{2};-w)})(\frac{-k_{1}-k_{2}-w}{2})\oplus H^{i-1}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(2-k_{1},k_{2};-w)})(\frac{-2+k_{1}-k_{2}-w}{2})

\oplus H^{i-2}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(3-k_{2},k_{1}+1;-w)})(\frac{-4+k_{2}-k_{1}-w}{2})\oplus H^{i-3}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(3-k_{2},3-k_{1};-w)})(\frac{-6+k_{2}+k_{1}-w}{2})

\mathrm{H}^{i}_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{\kappa,K_{p}})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=

H^{i}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(k_{1},k_{2};-w)}(-D_{K_{p}}))(\frac{-k_{1}-k_{2}-w}{2})\oplus

H^{i-1}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(2-k_{1},k_{2};-w)}(-D_{K_{p}}))(\frac{-2+k_{1}-k_{2}-w}{2})\oplus

H^{i-2}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(3-k_{2},k_{1}+1;-w)}(-D_{K_{p}}))(\frac{-4+k_{2}-k_{1}-w}{2})\oplus

H^{i-3}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\omega_{K_{p}}^{(3-k_{2},3-k_{1};-w)}(-D_{K_{p}}))(\frac{-6+k_{2}+k_{1}-w}{2})

We can also state a similar result using completed cohomology. We first recall the following theorem:

Theorem 4.8.3
.

Let
V_{\kappa}
be a finite dimensional representation of
G
of highest weight
\kappa
. Then

\displaystyle CC_{0}(V_{\kappa})=\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(V_{\kappa},\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{colim}_{K_{p}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{\kappa,K_{p}})

\displaystyle CC_{\mathrm{cusp},0}(V_{\kappa})=\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(V_{\kappa},\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{colim}_{K_{p}}\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K_{p}K^{p}},\mathcal{V}^{\vee}_{\kappa,K_{p}})

Proof.

See e.g.
[
Eme06
, Cor. 2.2.18]
. ∎

We deduce the following:

Corollary 4.8.4
.

We have that

\mathrm{H}^{i}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{g}}(V_{\kappa},\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})){\otimes}{\mathbf{C}}_{p}=

H^{i}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(k_{1},k_{2};-w),\mathrm{sm}})(\frac{-k_{1}-k_{2}-w}{2})\oplus H^{i-1}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2-k_{1},k_{2};-w),\mathrm{sm}})(\frac{-2+k_{1}-k_{2}-w}{2})

\oplus H^{i-2}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(3-k_{2},k_{1}+1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})(\frac{-4+k_{2}-k_{1}-w}{2})\oplus H^{i-3}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(3-k_{2},3-k_{1};-w),\mathrm{sm}})(\frac{-6+k_{2}+k_{1}-w}{2})

There is a similar statement for compactly supported cohomology and the cuspidal coherent cohomology.

Proof.

This is simply obtained by passing to the limit over
K_{p}
in
Theorem
4.8.2
. But alternatively, this is a combination of Theorem

4.7.1
, Lemma
4.6.5
and Proposition
3.5.17
.
∎

4.9.
The
p
-adic Eichler–Shimura theory for
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}

We continue to assume that
G=\GSp_{4}
, and let
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)_{E}
. We are ultimately interested in the case that

\lambda=(1,1;w)
. We will now specialize the results of Section

4.7
, in order to compute the Hodge–Tate structure of the
ordinary part of

CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})=\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})
. We will use some more
suggestive notation for the higher Coleman sheaves now that we specialize to

\mathrm{GSp}_{4}
. Let us briefly summarize who are the main players.

•

For all
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)^{M,+}
, we have the classical modular sheaves
\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}}
, computing classical cohomology.

•

Following Definition
4.6.6
, we have the “big” sheaves
\omega^{{\dagger},\kappa}_{w}
on
\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{w}^{\dagger})
for all
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
and all
w\in{}^{M}W
. If
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)^{M,+}
we have maps
\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{w}^{\dagger})}\rightarrow\omega^{{\dagger},\kappa}_{w}\otimes E(-w^{-1}w_{0,M}\kappa)
by Proposition
4.6.9
(where the twist is a twist of the
B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-action).

•

We have higher Coleman theories
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{{\dagger},\kappa}_{w})
for the big sheaves and
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,sm})
for the classical sheaves.

The superscript
(-)^{\ord}
means the ordinary part, which is the minimal
slope part; we caution the reader that precisely what the “minimal slope” part
occasionally depends on the context, but will always be spelled out. On
CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})
, the ordinary part is the slope
=0
part by Theorem
4.7.5
.
Theorem
4.7.5
specializes as follows.

Theorem 4.9.1
.

There is a spectral sequence:

E_{1}^{p,q}:\big(H^{2p+q-d}_{{}^{p}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{p}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{p}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(-{{}^{p}w}^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho-\rho)\big)^{\ord}\Rightarrow

H^{p+q}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p})

and similarly:

E_{1}^{p,q}:\big(H^{2p+q-d}_{{}^{p}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{p}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{p}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})}(-D))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(-{{}^{p}w}^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho-\rho)\big)^{\ord}\Rightarrow

H^{p+q}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}).

If the Shimura variety were proper, we could use Corollary
4.7.6
to simplify the spectral sequence. In our case we will arrive to a similar conclusion after making a non-Eisenstein localization. We can give a first analysis of the spectral sequence with the help of some vanishing theorems. The following lemma comes as a complement to Theorem
4.6.58
.

Lemma 4.9.2
.

(1)

For all
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)_{E}
, we have that

H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=0

for all
w\neq\mathrm{Id}
and

H^{3}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=H^{3}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=0

for all
w\neq w_{0}^{M}
.

(2)

For all
\kappa\in X^{*}(T)^{M,+}
, we have that

H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}}(-D))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=0

for all
w\neq\mathrm{Id}
and

H^{3}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}}(-D))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=H^{3}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}=0

for all
w\neq w_{0}^{M}
.

Proof.

The statements regarding
H^{0}
and
H^{3}
are equivalent under Serre duality (
[
BP21
, Thm. 6.7.2]
). The vanishing of
H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
for
w\neq\mathrm{Id}
follows from Theorem
4.6.58
. The injective map of sheaves
\omega^{{\dagger},\kappa}_{w}(-D)\rightarrow\omega^{{\dagger},\kappa}_{w}
induces an injective map
H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))\rightarrow H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})
which implies the vanishing of
H^{0}_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
for
w\neq\mathrm{Id}
. One argues similarly for the sheaf
\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}}
.
∎

Proposition 4.9.3
.

(1)

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}
is supported in degrees in the range
[1,3]
. Moreover
(with obvious notation) the graded pieces for the Hodge–Tate decompositions
are:

(a)

H^{3}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:\mathrm{coker}(H^{1}_{{}^{2}w}(-D)\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(-D)),H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(-D),H^{1}_{{}^{1}w}(-D),H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(-D)
.

(b)

H^{2}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:H^{2}_{{}^{3}w}(-D),\mathrm{Ker}(H^{1}_{{}^{2}w}(-D)\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(-D))
.

(c)

H^{1}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:H^{1}_{{}^{3}w}(-D)
.

(2)

\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},{\mathbf{Q}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}
is supported in degrees in the range
[3,5]
. Moreover
the graded for the Hodge–Tate decompositions are:

(a)

H^{3}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:H^{3}_{{}^{3}w},H^{2}_{{}^{2}w},H^{1}_{{}^{1}w},\mathrm{Ker}(H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{1}w})
.

(b)

H^{4}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:\mathrm{Coker}(H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{1}w}),H^{1}_{{}^{0}w}
.

(c)

H^{5}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}:H^{2}_{{}^{0}w}
.

Proof.

From Theorem
4.6.58
and Lemma
4.9.2
, we have that the cohomology
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
is supported in the range:

•

[0,2]
for
w=~^{0}w
,

•

[1,2]
for
w=~^{1}w
,

•

[2]
for
w=~^{2}w
,

•

[3]
for
w=~^{3}w
.

On the other hand, the cohomology
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{f\kern-0.49164pt{s}}
is supported in the range:

•

[0]
for
w=~^{0}w
,

•

[1]
for
w=~^{1}w
,

•

[1,2]
for
w=~^{2}w
,

•

[1,3]
for
w=~^{3}w
.

It follows that the spectral sequences of Theorem
4.9.1

degenerate on the second page.
∎

We now fix an irreducible residual representation
\bar{\rho}:G_{\mathbf{Q}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{p})
. We define a maximal ideal
\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}

of the abstract spherical Hecke algebra of level prime to
S\cup\{p\}
(where
S
is
the set of primes at which
\overline{\rho}
is ramified or
K^{p}
is not hyperspecial) by the formula:

P_{\ell}(X)\mod\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}=\mathrm{det}(X-\bar{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell})),~\ell\notin S\cup\{p\}

where
P_{\ell}(X)
is the Hecke polynomial defined in (
1.8.27
).

Theorem 4.9.4
.

The map
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
is a quasi-isomorphism.

Proof.

Indeed the cohomology of the boundary is Eisenstein by identical
arguments to those of
[
NT16
, §4]
.
∎

Corollary 4.9.5
.

The maps
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
are quasi-isomorphisms.

Proof.

While this could be proved by analyzing the cohomology
of the boundary, we argue as follows. By Proposition

4.9.3
and Theorem
4.9.4
, it suffices to show that
the maps

H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{0}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})})^{\ord}\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{1}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{1}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{1}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})})^{\ord},

H^{1}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{2}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})}(-D))^{\ord}\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{3}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})}(-D))^{\ord}

are
0
after
localizing at
\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}
. The second statement follows
from the first by duality, and the first statement follows from the fact
that the natural map

H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}

is an isomorphism, which is Lemma
4.9.6
below.
∎

Lemma 4.9.6
.

The map
H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},\kappa}(-D))^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},\kappa})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

This is similar to
[
Pil20
, Cor. 15.2.3.1]
and
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 3.10.7]
, except that we work with ordinary
p
-adic modular forms rather than classical forms. We translate the statement to a result about Hida complexes which can then be proved as in these previous results but working mod
p
and with the structure sheaf.

The Hida complexes we consider are constructed in
[
BP23
]
and
also recalled below in Section
7.3
; they are perfect complexes

M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathrm{cusp}}
and
M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w}

of
\Lambda={\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket
-modules, and there is a natural morphism
M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathrm{cusp}}\to M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w}
. The complex
M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathrm{cusp}}
is projective in degree
0 (and in fact it is the classical object constructed by Hida

[
Hid02
]
), while
M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w}
has amplitude
[0,2]
. We can also
consider the boundary Hida complex

M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\partial}=\mathrm{cone}(M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathrm{cusp}}\to M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w})
. A priori this has amplitude
[-1,2]
but we will recall
below the simple geometric reason that it has amplitude
[0,2]
.

Below we shall prove that after non Eisenstein localization

M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\partial,\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}
has amplitude
[1,2]
(in
fact it actually vanishes, but since we do not need this, we do not prove it).
This statement implies
that for any continuous homomorphism
\nu:T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})\to\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
the morphism

H^{0}(M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathrm{cusp},\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}\otimes^{L}_{\Lambda,\nu}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})\to H^{0}(M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}\otimes^{L}_{\Lambda,\nu}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})

(4.9.7)

is an isomorphism, and by the comparison between higher Hida and Coleman theory
[
BP23
, Thm 6.2.15]
and Theorem
4.6.56
this is exactly the statement of the lemma.

To prove the claim, by Nakayama’s lemma it suffices to prove that

H^{0}(M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathrm{cusp},\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}\otimes^{L}_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{F}}_{p}[T({\mathbf{F}}_{p})])\to H^{0}(M^{\bullet}_{{}^{0}w,\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}\otimes^{L}_{\Lambda}{\mathbf{F}}_{p}[T({\mathbf{F}}_{p})])

is an isomorphism. We now translate this back into a statement about mod
p
modular forms on the ordinary locus which we prove by analyzing the boundary.

We consider
IG/{\mathbf{F}}_{p}
, the special fiber of the (ordinary) Igusa variety
corresponding to the pro-
p
Iwahori subgroup of
P^{\prime}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
, in the notation of

[
BP23
, §3.4.5]
. We let
\pi:IG^{\mathrm{tor}}\to IG^{*}
be its
(partial) toroidal and minimal compactifications. By the very construction of
the Hida complexes, the map (
4.9.7
) is nothing but the natural map

H^{0}(IG^{\mathrm{tor}},\mathcal{O}(-D))^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}\to H^{0}(IG^{\mathrm{tor}},\mathcal{O})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}}}

(4.9.8)

(For this and the meaning of the ordinary part, see
[
BP23
, §5.2]
, but note that the setup is substantially simplified
because
w={}^{0}w
.) We remark that even without the non-Eisenstein localization this map is
always injective, which justifies the assertion made above that the boundary cohomology always has amplitude
[0,2]
.

We now follow the strategy of
[
Pil20
, Cor. 15.2.3.1]
and

[
BCGP21
, Lem. 3.10.7]
to prove that (
4.9.8
) is surjective
after non-Eisenstein localization. If not then there is a non-Eisenstein
Hecke eigenvector occurring in
H^{0}(IG^{\mathrm{tor}},{\mathcal{O}}_{D})
. We write

D^{*}\subseteq IG^{*}
for the (reduced) boundary. We have

\pi_{*}{\mathcal{O}}_{D}={\mathcal{O}}_{D^{*}}
so that

H^{0}(D,{\mathcal{O}}_{D})=H^{0}(D^{*},{\mathcal{O}}_{D^{*}})
. The boundary
D^{*}
is a union of (ordinary) Igusa curves crossing at cusps. We write
\tilde{D}^{*}
for the normalization, which is a disjoint union of ordinary Igusa curves. We have an injective pullback map

H^{0}(D^{*},{\mathcal{O}}_{D^{*}})\to H^{0}(\tilde{D}^{*},{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{D}^{*}})

There is a compatibility between the
\GSp_{4}
Hecke action and the
\GL_{2}

Hecke action at primes away from
p
and the tame level (see
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 3.10.7]
for a precise statement). This implies that the systems of Hecke
eigenvalues in
H^{0}(\tilde{D}^{*},{\mathcal{O}}_{\tilde{D}^{*}})
are Eisenstein,
as required. ∎

Theorem 4.9.9
.

For any irreducible representation
\bar{\rho}:G_{\mathbf{Q}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{p})
, the localization

V:=\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}^{\ord}=CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}^{\ord}

is concentrated in degree
3
.
Moreover, there is a
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\times\mathbf{T}_{K^{p}}\times T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-equivariant filtration
\{Fil^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}\}_{i=0,1,2,3}
on
V\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}
and

\mathrm{Gr}^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}=\big(H^{i}_{{}^{i}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{i}w}^{{\dagger},-w_{0,M}({{}^{i}w}\cdot\lambda+2\rho^{M})})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(-{{}^{i}w}^{-1}w_{0,M}\rho-\rho)\big)_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}^{\ord}.

The Sen operator is scalar on
\mathrm{Gr}^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
and acts via
\frac{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-w}{2}
,
\frac{-2+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-w}{2}
,
\frac{-4+\lambda_{2}-\lambda_{1}-w}{2}
,
\frac{-6+\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-w}{2}
for
i=3,2,1,0
respectively.

Proof.

This is immediate from Proposition
4.9.3
, Theorem
4.9.4
and Corollary
4.9.5
.
∎

4.10.
Sen and Cousin

We now specialize to
\lambda=(1,1;w)
.

4.10.1.
The Cousin map for the classical sheaves

All the action will be happening on the union of Bruhat strata (in fact a
Q
-orbit)
C_{{}^{3}w,Q}=C_{{}^{3}w}\cup C_{{}^{2}w}
.
We have an extension over
\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})
, corresponding to the stratification of
C_{{}^{3}w,Q}
into
B
-orbits, with
j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}:\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w})\hookrightarrow\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})
:

0\rightarrow(j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})_{!}\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w})}\rightarrow\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})}\rightarrow\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{2}w})}\rightarrow 0

(4.10.2)

Proposition 4.10.3
.

(1)

The natural map:

\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q}),\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})

induces a quasi-isomorphism on the ordinary part (the slope
=-(1,1;w)
-part).

(2)

Moreover,
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{c}(\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q}),\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}
is computed by the following complex in degrees
2,3
where
Cous
is induced by the class of the extension (
4.10.2
)

H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Cous}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.6.13
, we have a
spectral sequence (the Cousin spectral sequence) from local
cohomologies converging to classical cohomology. The first statement is
equivalent to the vanishing of the ordinary part of the higher Coleman
theories for the elements
{}^{0}w
and
{}^{1}w
. By Theorem

4.6.60
, we find that the slopes on
H^{i}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})
are
\geq-(2,2;w)
and the slopes on

H^{i}_{{}^{1}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})
are
\geq(0,-2;-w)
. Since
-(1,1;w)+\gamma=-(2,2;w)
and
-(1,1;w)+\alpha=(0,-2;-w)
we conclude that the ordinary part vanishes.
The second statement is a consequence of Theorem
4.6.58
and Lemma
4.9.2
.
∎

4.10.4.
The Cousin map for the big sheaves

Applying the functor
VB^{0}
to the sheaf
HCS_{Q,^{3}w,\eta}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda})
of (
3.6.5
) and twisting the
B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-action by
\lambda-\eta
yields an extension over

\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})
:

0\rightarrow j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\rightarrow VB^{0}(HCS_{Q,^{3}w,\eta}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda}))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(\lambda-\eta)\rightarrow\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0;0))\rightarrow 0

(4.10.5)

The natural map in
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w},\mathfrak{b}_{M_{{}^{3}w}})
(mapping
a Verma of dominant weight to its finite dimensional quotient)

M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda}\rightarrow L(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda}
yields
a map

HCS_{Q,^{3}w,\eta}(L(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda})\rightarrow HCS_{Q,^{3}w,\eta}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda}).

As in
Lemma
4.6.5
, applying
VB^{0}
gives a
map

\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(\eta)|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})}\rightarrow VB^{0}(HCS_{Q,^{3}w,\eta}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda})).

We deduce that there is a map of extensions from (
4.10.2
) to (
4.10.5
) (for clarity we drop the twist of the

B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-action in this diagram):

\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{VB^{0}(HCS_{Q,^{3}w,\eta}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{\lambda}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{(j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})_{!}\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{2}w})}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

Proposition 4.10.6
.

The maps

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((1,1;w))^{\ord}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0;0))^{\ord}

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((1,1;w))^{\ord}

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}

are quasi-isomorphisms.

Consequently we have a quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((1,1;w))^{\ord}=

H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0;0))^{\ord}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Cous}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}

where the complex is in degree
[2,3]
and the map
Cous
is induced by the class of the extension (
4.10.5
).

Proof.

We have the BGG short exact sequence

0\rightarrow M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(0,2;w)}\rightarrow M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(1,1;w)}\rightarrow L(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(1,1;w)}\rightarrow 0.

Applying
HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}
gives a triangle

HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}(L(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(1,1;w)})\rightarrow HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(1,1;w)})\rightarrow HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(0,2;w)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

By Theorem
4.6.60
, the ordinary part of
HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(0,2;w)})
is trivial, so that we get a quasi-isomorphism
HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}(L(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(1,1;w)}))^{\ord}\rightarrow HC_{{}^{3}w,\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{m}_{{}^{3}w})_{(1,1;w)})^{\ord}
.
This translates into the quasi-isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((1,1;w))^{\ord}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}
.
The quasi-isomorphism
\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((1,1;w))^{\ord}\rightarrow\mathrm{R}\Gamma_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0;0))^{\ord}
follows by similar considerations.
The second part is then
immediate from Proposition
4.10.3
.∎

Remark 4.10.7
.

We also have a quasi-isomorphism

\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}}(-D))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,2;w))^{\ord}=

H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}(-D))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((3,3;0))^{\ord}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Cous}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{1}_{{}^{1}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{1}w}^{{\dagger},(2,2;-w)}(-D))\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((-1,3;0))^{\ord}

where the complex is in degrees
[0,1]
.
This statement is Serre dual to Proposition
4.10.3
.

4.10.8.
The Sen map

Let
V=H^{3}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}^{\ord})
. By Theorem
4.9.9
,
V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
carries a filtration where

•

\mathrm{Gr}^{3}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}=H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(0,0;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
,

•

\mathrm{Gr}^{2}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}=H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(2,0;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
,

•

\mathrm{Gr}^{1}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}=H^{1}_{{}^{1}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{1}w}^{{\dagger},(2,2;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(-1,3;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
,

•

\mathrm{Gr}^{0}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}=H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},(2,2;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(3,3;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}
.

Acting on
V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
we have a Sen operator whose eigenvalues are
-1-\frac{w}{2},-1-\frac{w}{2},-2-\frac{w}{2},-2-\frac{w}{2}
.
The generalized Hodge–Tate weight
1+\frac{w}{2}
-part of
V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}
fits in the following short exact sequence (where
\Theta
is the Sen operator and we drop the twist of the
B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-action to lighten the notation):

0\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow V_{{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}}[(\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2})^{2}]\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow 0.

(4.10.9)

Since
Sen:=\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2}
is nilpotent, it induces a map:

H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(2,0;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Sen}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}.

(4.10.10)

Similarly, by looking at the weight
2+\frac{w}{2}
-part of
V
we obtain the following map:

H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{0}w}^{{\dagger},(2,2;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(3,3;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Sen}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{1}_{{}^{1}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{1}w}^{{\dagger},(2,2;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(-1,3;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}

4.10.11.
Comparison between the Sen and Cousin map

The following theorem is one of the main results in this section. It is a
generalization of
[
Pan22a
, Thm. 5.3.18]
, in the modular curve case. We
will follow the method of proof of
[
Pil24
, Thm. 6.1]
.

Theorem 4.10.12
.

The two maps

Cous,Sen:H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}(2,0;0)^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\bar{\rho}}}

(coming respectively from
Proposition
4.10.6
and (
4.10.10
))agree up to a non-zero scalar.

Proof.

We consider
{\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}C_{{}^{3}w,Q}}=VB^{\operatorname{red}}(\operatorname{Loc}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}C_{{}^{3}w,Q}})
, which fits in the triangle (obtained by applying
VB^{\operatorname{red}}
to Proposition
3.6.6
):

\Ext^{0}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})})\rightarrow{\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})})

\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})})[-1]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

where

\mathrm{Ext}^{0}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}C_{{}^{3}w,Q}})=(j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})_{!}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}
and
\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}C_{{}^{3}w,Q}})=\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0,0))
.

On
{\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})})
, we
have
(\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2})^{2}=0
.
Let us introduce some simplifying notations and denote by:

•

X=(j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})_{!}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}
,

•

Y={\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2}}(\lambda,\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})})
,

•

Z=\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0,0))[-1]
,

•

W={\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2},\Theta}(\lambda,-1-\frac{w}{2},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}|_{\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})})
.

Applying
[
Sta13
,
Tag 05R0
]
to the commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2})}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y}

we obtain the following commutative diagram, where all lines and columns are part of distinguished triangles:

\textstyle{X\oplus X[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{W\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\oplus Z[-1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X[1]\oplus X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2})}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{X[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{0}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{Z\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{X[1]}

The top horizontal triangle can be written as

(j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})_{!}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\oplus(j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})_{!}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}[-1]\rightarrow{\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2},\Theta}(\lambda,-1-\frac{w}{2},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})

\rightarrow\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}[-1]\oplus\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}[-2]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle+1}}{{\rightarrow}}

(compare (
3.6.7
); we again drop the twist of the
B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
-action for simplicity and we note that taking the
\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2}
-cohomology localizes over
\pi_{HT}^{-1}(C_{{}^{3}w,Q})
so we also drop it from the notation.)

Taking cohomology yields a long exact sequence:

H^{3}({\mathrm{RHom}}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2},\Theta}(\lambda,-1-\frac{w}{2},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}))\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\delta}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)})

where the map
\delta
is induced by the class of the extension (extracted from the top horizontal triangle):

0\rightarrow j_{{}^{3}w,\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}}\omega_{{}^{3}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\rightarrow\mathrm{Ext}^{1}_{\mathfrak{b},*_{2},\Theta}(\lambda,-1-\frac{w}{2},\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{la}}_{\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}}})\rightarrow\omega_{{}^{2}w}^{{\dagger},(1,1;-w)}\otimes{\mathbf{C}}_{p}((2,0;0))\rightarrow 0.

(4.10.13)

By definition, the map
Sen
is equal to
\delta
.
Now, by Theorem
3.6.9
and Theorem
4.5.20
, the extensions
(
4.10.5
) and (
4.10.13
) agree up to a non-zero
scalar. Thus the maps
Cous,Sen
agree up to a non-zero
scalar, as claimed.
∎

4.11.
The Eichler–Shimura relation and
semi-simplicity

The following is a special case of a result of Nekovář,

[
Nek18
]
. If
r
is a finite-dimensional representation of a
group
\Gamma
, and
g\in\Gamma
, then we write
\operatorname{char}_{r(g)}
for the
characteristic polynomial of
r(g)
.

Proposition 4.11.1
.

Let
\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
and
s:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GL_{n}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
be continuous representations
(
for some

n\geq 1
)
and assume that

(1)

the Zariski closure of
\rho(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})

contains
\Sp_{4}
, and

(2)

for a density one set of primes
l
, we have

\operatorname{char}_{\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_{l})}\bigl(s(\mathrm{Frob}_{l})\bigr)=0
.

Then we have
s\cong\rho^{\oplus m}
for some
integer
m\geq 1
.

Proof.

We claim that the result is an immediate application
of
[
Nek18
, Prop. 3.10]
(with
\ell
replaced by
p
),
taking
\Gamma=\Gamma^{\prime}=G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
,
a=r=1
, and
the representations
\rho
,
\rho_{1}
of
[
Nek18
, Prop. 3.10]
to
be
s
and
\rho
respectively.

The hypothesis (C’) of
[
Nek18
, Prop. 3.10]
is immediate
from our hypotheses, taking
\Sigma
to be the set of
the
\mathrm{Frob}_{l}
for primes
l
satisfying Condition (
2
). For hypothesis (A’), since the Zariski closure
of
\rho(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})
contains
\Sp_{4}
by hypothesis (and is contained
in
\GSp_{4}
),
the Lie algebra
\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\cdot{\operatorname{Lie}\,}(\rho(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}))
is
equal to
\mathfrak{sp}_{4}
or
\mathfrak{gsp}_{4}
,
and the representation of
this Lie algebra induced by
\rho
is the standard
4
-dimensional
representation, which is minuscule. We are thus in the situation of
part (3) of
[
Nek18
, Prop. 3.10]
, and the proposition follows.
∎

We have the following variant of Proposition
4.11

in the induced case:

Proposition 4.11.2
.

Let
\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
and
s:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GL_{n}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
be continuous representations and assume that

(1)

the Zariski closure of
\rho(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})

contains
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
,

(2)

\rho
is absolutely irreducible but becomes reducible
on some index two subgroup
G_{E}
, and

(3)

for a density one set of primes
l
, we have

\operatorname{char}_{\rho(\mathrm{Frob}_{l})}\bigl(s(\mathrm{Frob}_{l})\bigr)=0
.

Then we have
s\cong\rho^{\oplus m}
for some
integer
m\geq 1
.

Proof.

We may write
\rho|_{G_{E}}=\varrho\oplus\varrho^{c}
, where
\Gal(E/{\mathbf{Q}})
permutes the factors.
The assumption that
\rho
is symplectic (together with the assumptions on the
image of
\rho
)
implies that
\det(\varrho)
and
\det(\varrho^{c})

are both the restriction to
G_{E}
of the similitude character of
\rho
.

Let
t
be an irreducible subquotient of
s|_{G_{E}}
.
Assumption (
3
) implies that we have
\operatorname{char}_{\rho(h)}\bigl(t(h)\bigr)=0

for a dense set of elements
h\in G_{E}
.
We may assume that
\varrho
,
\varrho^{c}
, and
t

all have models over the ring of integers
{\mathcal{O}}
of some finite extension of
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}
.
Let
T
and
P
denote the
Zariski closures of
t
and
\varrho\oplus\varrho^{c}
respectively.
By assumption,
T
is reductive, and
the Zariski closure of
t\oplus\varrho\oplus\varrho^{c}
inside
T\oplus P
is also reductive,
and by Goursat’s lemma is the graph of some projections
\pi_{1}:T\rightarrow G
,

\pi_{2}:P\rightarrow G
onto a common quotient
G
.
By the Chebotarev density theorem and continuity,
Assumption (
3
) implies that the minimal polynomial of any element in
K=\ker(\pi_{1})
divides
(X-1)^{4}
. This is because elements in the image of
s

which lie in
K
are limits of
s(\mathrm{Frob}_{l})
for Frobenius elements
\mathrm{Frob}_{l}
, and
by assumption these will satisfy
(s(\mathrm{Frob}_{l})-1)^{4}\mathcal{O}^{n}\subset\pi^{m}\mathcal{O}^{n}
for larger
and larger
m
.
This implies that
K
is
unipotent, which — since
T
is reductive — implies that
K
is trivial.
Hence
G=T
and thus
T
is a quotient of
P
. But now from the fact that
P

contains
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
, we see that the only possibilities for
t

up to twist are
\Sym^{i}\varrho\otimes\Sym^{j}\varrho^{c}
, from which one easily sees that
t

must either be
\varrho
or
\varrho^{c}
, and thus
any irreducible subquotient of
s|_{G_{E}}

is either
\varrho
or
\varrho^{c}
.

We claim that
s|_{G_{E}}
cannot contain any non-trivial extensions of
\varrho
by
\varrho
(equally,
of
\varrho^{c}
by
\varrho^{c}
). To see this, note that a generic element in the image of
\varrho^{c}

acts invertibly on
\varrho
. Hence the assumptions imply that for of a dense
set of
h\in G_{E}
, the characteristic polynomial of
\varrho(h)

annihilates this extension of
\varrho
by
\varrho
.
But then the results follow from the
\GL_{2}
-version
of
[
Nek18
, Prop. 3.10]
(first proved in
[
BLR91
]
).

Now return to representations of
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
.
By what we have shown for
s|_{G_{E}}
, we deduce that every irreducible
subquotient of
s
is isomorphic to
\rho
.
Hence it suffices to rule out the case that
s
is of the form

0\rightarrow\rho\rightarrow W\rightarrow\rho\rightarrow 0

for some non-split extension
W
.
Note that the restriction
W|_{G_{E}}
is an extension:

0\rightarrow\varrho\oplus\varrho^{c}\rightarrow W|_{G_{E}}\rightarrow\varrho\oplus\varrho^{c}\rightarrow 0.

In particular,
W|_{G_{E}}
corresponds to a
\Gal(E/{\mathbf{Q}})
-invariant class in

\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho,\varrho^{c})\oplus\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho,\varrho)\oplus\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho^{c},\varrho^{c})\oplus\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho^{c},\varrho).

As already shown, the corresponding extensions of
\varrho
by
\varrho
and
\varrho^{c}
by
\varrho^{c}

both split, so
the projection of this class to both
\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho^{c},\varrho)

and
\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho,\varrho^{c})
(they are permuted by the Galois
action) must be non-trivial.
In particular,
we may assume that
W|_{G_{E}}

has a subquotient which is
a genuine extension of
\varrho
by
\varrho^{c}
. Our assumptions on the Zariski closure of the image of
\rho
imply that a generic element of
\rho

is regular semi-simple. Thus, by Chebotarev and assumption (
3
), it follows
that there is a dense set of elements of
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
which act semi-simply on
W
.
Moreover, since the tensor product of two semi-simple matrices is semi-simple, the same holds
for the tensor product
W\otimes\rho^{\vee}
and so consequently also for any subquotient of this representation.
We have an exact sequence:

0\rightarrow\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee}\rightarrow W\otimes\rho^{\vee}\rightarrow\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee}\rightarrow 0.

(4.11.3)

Write
\chi
for the quadratic character of
\Gal(E/{\mathbf{Q}})
,
and let
\eta
be the similitude character of
\rho
. We have (compare
[
BCGP21
, §7.5.16]
) a decomposition

\rho\otimes\rho^{\vee}\simeq\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\oplus\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}(\chi)\oplus\Ind^{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}}_{G_{E}}\operatorname{ad}^{0}(\varrho)\oplus\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\oplus\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\otimes\chi,

where
\char 115\relax(\varrho)
is the Asai representation (i.e. the tensor induction).
Hence taking suitable subquotients of (
4.11.3
), we arrive at a pair of extensions

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\rightarrow U\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\rightarrow 0,

(4.11.4)

\displaystyle 0\rightarrow\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\otimes\chi\rightarrow V\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}\rightarrow 0.

We claim that at least one of these sequences must be non-split. The point is that

\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho,\varrho^{c})=H^{1}(E,\Hom(\varrho,\varrho^{c})),

but
\Hom(\varrho,\varrho^{c})
is the restriction of
\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}
to
G_{E}
, and thus,
by Shapiro’s lemma, we have

\displaystyle\Ext^{1}_{G_{E}}(\varrho,\varrho^{c})

\displaystyle=H^{1}(E,\left(\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\right)|_{G_{E}})

\displaystyle=H^{1}({\mathbf{Q}},\left(\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\right)\otimes\Ind^{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}}_{G_{E}}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})

\displaystyle=H^{1}({\mathbf{Q}},\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}\otimes\chi)\oplus H^{1}({\mathbf{Q}},\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1});

and by construction, the elements of
the right hand side corresponding to our non-split extension of
\varrho

by
\varrho^{c}
coming from
W|_{G_{E}}
are the extensions
U,V
of (
4.11.4
).
We consider the case that
U
is non-split, the case of
V
being entirely similar.

To complete the proof, it suffices to show that for any such non-split extension, there cannot
be a dense set of
g\in G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
which act semi-simply.
Write
A=\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}
, and let
G
and
H
be the Zariski closures of the images of
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}

and
G_{E}
on
U
respectively.
By constriction, the Zariski closure of the image of
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}
on
A
is the orthogonal
group
\mathrm{O}_{4}
, and the Zariski closure of the image of
G_{E}
on
A

is the index two subgroup
\mathrm{SO}_{4}
which is isomorphic to the image of
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
.
Any generic
h\in G_{E}

will
act semi-simply on
U
because it will
have distinct eigenvalues. However, any
g\in G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\smallsetminus G_{E}
has
1
as an eigenvalue on
both
A=\char 115\relax(\varrho)\otimes\eta^{-1}
and
A\otimes\chi

(the eigenvalues in either case take the form
1,-1,\lambda,-\lambda
for
some
\lambda
), so that in particular the eigenvalues of
g
on
U
are contained within the eigenvalues of
g
on
A
.
By our semi-simplicity hypothesis, there is therefore a dense set of such
g

with the property that the image of
g
in
\End(U)
is annihilated
by the characteristic polynomial of
g
on
A
. By continuity, this extends to
all
elements
of
G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\smallsetminus G_{E}
and also to all elements of
G\smallsetminus H
.

Now,
G
is a subgroup of the semi-direct product
N\rtimes\mathrm{O}_{4}
,
where
N
is the standard representation of
\mathrm{O}_{4}
of dimension
4
;
and the projection
G\to\mathrm{O}_{4}
is surjective. Since
N
is abelian, the
conjugation action of
G
on
N
factors through this surjection, and since
N

is an irreducible representation of
\mathrm{O}_{4}
, we see that
either
G=\mathrm{O}_{4}
(in which case the extension (
4.11.4
)
splits, and we are done), or
G=N\rtimes\mathrm{O}_{4}
, in which case there are
elements
g\in G\smallsetminus H
whose minimal polynomial has degree
5
, a contradiction.
∎

4.12.
A classicality theorem

Let
S
be the finite set of primes at which
K^{p}

is not hyperspecial, together with the prime
p
. In this section we will consider an ordinary overconvergent modular form

f\in H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}
, which is an
eigenform for
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
as well as for the spherical Hecke operators at the
places not contained in
S
. We write
\chi_{f}^{sm}:T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})\rightarrow\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}^{\times}
for the smooth character corresponding to
f
. Via the
identification of dual groups,
\chi_{f}^{sm}
induces a cocharacter

{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow T(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
that we denote by
t\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(\chi_{1}(t),\chi_{2}(t),\chi_{3}(t),\chi_{4}(t))
.
We write

\mathfrak{m}_{f}\subseteq\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}[T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})]\otimes\bigotimes^{\prime}_{l\not\in S}\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/\kern-1.99997pt{/}{\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})}]

for the maximal ideal corresponding to
f
.

Lemma 4.12.1
.

We have a continuous semi-simple
Galois representation
\rho_{f}:G_{\mathbf{Q}}\to\GSp_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
, which satisfies the
following properties (where
P_{\ell}(X)
is as in (
1.8.27
)):

(1)

\rho_{f}
is unramified at primes
\ell\notin S
, and

P_{\ell}(X)\mod\mathfrak{m}_{f}=\det(X-\rho_{f}(\mathrm{Frob}_{\ell})).

(2)

\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
can be conjugated to a representation

\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}\rightarrow B(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
where the diagonal is given, via class field theory, by the
cocharacter

z\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(\chi^{-1}_{4}(z)z^{-1-\frac{w}{2}},\chi^{-1}_{3}(z)z^{-1-\frac{w}{2}},\chi^{-1}_{2}(z)^{-1}z^{-2-\frac{w}{2}},\chi_{1}^{-1}(z)^{-1}z^{-2-\frac{w}{2}})

for
z\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
,
and

p\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(\chi^{-1}_{4}(p)p^{-1-\frac{w}{2}},\chi^{-1}_{3}(p)p^{-1-\frac{w}{2}},\chi^{-1}_{2}(p)^{-1}p^{-2-\frac{w}{2}},\chi^{-1}_{1}(p)^{-1}p^{-2-\frac{w}{2}}).

Proof.

Note that any cohomological,
C
-algebraic automorphic representation
\pi
has an associated Galois representation

\rho_{\pi,p}
(see Section
1.8.12
). This Galois representation is
furthermore ordinary if
\pi_{p}
is ordinary. By a standard
argument using
p
-adic families, we can interpolate the Galois representations
\rho_{\pi,p}
associated
to
\pi
of regular weight (see Section
1.8.12
),
and we define
\rho_{f}
to be the representation corresponding to the
interpolation of
\rho_{\pi,p}^{\vee}\otimes\varepsilon^{-3}
. ∎

Remark 4.12.2
.

The reason for considering
\rho_{f}
rather than
\rho_{f}^{\vee}\otimes\varepsilon^{-3}
is that
\rho_{f}
is the Galois representation we are likely to realize in the completed cohomology, in view of Theorem
1.8.29
.

We
will also assume that
\overline{\rho}_{f}
is irreducible and write

\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}
for the corresponding maximal ideal of the spherical
Hecke algebra with
\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{p}
coefficients, as in the previous sections. We let
V=H^{3}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}})
, but we think of this space
as
CC_{0}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}
and not as

CC_{\lambda}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}
. In other words we now twist the
B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
action to make it smooth (and not
\lambda
-smooth as in section
4.10
).

Lemma 4.12.3
.

Let
f\in H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}
be an ordinary overconvergent modular eigenform with Galois representation

\rho_{f}:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{f}
be the corresponding maximal ideal.
We assume that
\overline{\rho}_{f}
is irreducible and either

(1)

the Zariski closure of
\rho_{f}(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})
contains
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}
; or

(2)

the Zariski closure of
\rho_{f}(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})

contains
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
, and
\rho
is irreducible but becomes reducible
on some index two subgroup
G_{E}
.

Let
V=H^{3}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}})
.
Then
V[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=\rho_{f}\otimes_{\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}}W
for some finite-dimensional
vector space
W\neq 0
.

Proof.

Note that
V
carries a global Galois action since by results recalled in
4.4
,
\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})=\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}^{alg}_{K^{p}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
. By Theorem
4.9.9
,

V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}:=V\otimes_{\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}}{\mathbf{C}}_{p}
has a decreasing filtration with graded pieces

\{\mathrm{Gr}^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}\}_{i=0,1,2,3}
which are certain explicit spaces of
ordinary higher Coleman theories localized at
\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}
. In
particular (bearing in mind Proposition
4.10.6
),

\mathrm{Gr}^{0}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}=(H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}}))^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}.

Let
\chi
be the Nebentypus of
f
(the finite order character giving the action of
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
on
f
). Then each
\mathrm{Gr}^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[\chi]
is finite dimensional (since we are
fixing the slope to be ordinary, and also the action of
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
). Taking the
\chi
-isotypic part is an exact operation for smooth
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
-modules in characteristic
0
. We deduce that
V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[\chi]
is finite-dimensional and has a filtration with graded pieces the

\{\mathrm{Gr}^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[\chi]\}_{i=0,1,2,3}
.
Since
H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}})[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]\neq 0
, this implies that

V[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]\neq 0
is finite dimensional. The result follows from the Eichler–Shimura relation (Theorem
1.8.29
)
together with Proposition
4.11.1
and
Corollary
4.11.2
.∎

By Lemma
4.12.1
, the representation
\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
is ordinary,
i.e.
\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
preserves a Borel. The representation
\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
is de Rham if it fits in an extension:

0\rightarrow\rho_{f}^{(1+\frac{w}{2})}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}\rightarrow\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}\rightarrow\rho^{(2+\frac{w}{2})}_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}\rightarrow 0

where

\rho_{f}^{(1+\frac{w}{2})}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}(1+\frac{w}{2})
and

\rho^{(2+\frac{w}{2})}_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}(2+\frac{w}{2})
are potentially
unramified
2
-dimensional representations. Equivalently, this means that the
Sen operator of
D_{Sen}(\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}})
is semi-simple with
eigenvalues
-1-\frac{w}{2}
and
-2-\frac{w}{2}
.

Theorem 4.12.4
.

Let
f\in H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}
be an ordinary overconvergent modular eigenform with Galois representation

\rho_{f}:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
. Let
\mathfrak{m}_{f}

be the corresponding maximal ideal. Let
V=H^{3}(\mathrm{RHom}_{\mathfrak{b}}(\lambda,\mathrm{R}\Gamma(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})^{\mathrm{la}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}})
. We assume that:

(1)

Either

(a)

the Zariski closure of
\rho_{f}(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})
contains
\mathrm{Sp}_{4}
; or

(b)

the Zariski closure of
\rho_{f}(G_{{\mathbf{Q}}})

contains
\SL_{2}\times\SL_{2}
, and
\rho_{f}
is irreducible but becomes reducible
on some index two subgroup
G_{E}
.

(2)

The representation
\rho_{f}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
is de Rham.

(3)

There exists an integer
n
such that
\dim_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}\mathrm{Gr}^{i}V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=n
for each
0\leq i\leq 3
, and
\dim_{\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}}V[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=4n
.

(4)

The representation
\overline{\rho}_{f}
is irreducible.

Then
f
is a classical modular form.

Remark 4.12.5
.

The simplest way to verify the assumption
(3)
is to
prove the multiplicity one statement that
\mathrm{dim}_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}H^{i}_{{}^{i}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}})[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=1
for each
i
(when this statement is true). This forces
\dim_{\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}}V[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=4
. Indeed,
\dim_{\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p}}V[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]
is a multiple of
4
by Lemma
4.12.3
.

Proof.

By Proposition
4.10.6

and (
4.10.9
), we have an exact sequence (where
\Theta
is the arithmetic Sen
operator):

0\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}\rightarrow V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[(\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2})^{2}]\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}\rightarrow 0

Since
\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2}
is nilpotent, it induces a map:

H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}_{\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\rho}_{f}}}.

We pass to
\mathfrak{m}_{f}
-isotypic components.
Assumption (
3
) implies that the sequence:

0\rightarrow H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]\rightarrow V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[(\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2})^{2}][\mathfrak{m}_{f}]\rightarrow H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]\rightarrow 0

remains exact. Therefore, we get a map

H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Theta+1+\frac{w}{2}}}{{\rightarrow}}H^{3}_{{}^{3}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}].

The kernel of this map is the space of classical forms
H^{2}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]
because of Theorem

4.10.12
and Proposition
4.10.6
. The map is zero since
V_{{\mathbf{C}}_{p}}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=D_{Sen}(\rho_{f})^{\oplus n}
by Lemma
4.12.3
, and
\Theta

identifies with the Sen operator of
\rho_{f}
which is semi-simple by assumption.
It follows that
H^{2}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord})[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=H^{2}_{{}^{2}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(1,1;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]
. This implies that
\dim H^{0}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}}))[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=n
(by the
stability of the
L
-packet of automorphic forms corresponding to
f
) and
therefore
H^{0}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}}))[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]=H^{0}_{{}^{0}w}(\mathrm{Sh}^{\mathrm{tor}}_{K^{p}},\omega^{(2,2;-w),\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord}[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]
,
and we are done. ∎

Remark 4.12.6
.

The way we have explained the argument, we naturally proved the
classicality of the relevant space of degree
2
cohomology classes, and used
Arthur’s classification of automorphic forms to deduce the classicality of

f
. This may seem a bit strange. We should first explain why we focused on
degree
2
and degree
3
cohomology classes in the argument, instead of
degree
0
and degree
1
cohomology classes. The reason is that the
localization
\operatorname{Loc}(M(\mathfrak{g})_{\lambda})
is simpler on
C_{{}^{3}w,Q}
, but
has some richer structure on its complement. This rich structure is
irrelevant for the ordinary case, but would cause minor technical problems in
Section
4.10
.

Still focusing on degree
2
and
3
cohomology as we did, we could also have
proven the classicality of the relevant space of degree
3
cohomology, and then used
Serre duality instead of Arthur’s classification to deduce the classicality of

f
. In order to do this, one would consider
V/\mathfrak{m}_{f}V
instead of
V[\mathfrak{m}_{f}]
, and the same argument would apply with minor modifications.

5.
An ordinary modularity lifting theorem for unitary groups with
p=2

Our goal in this section is to prove Theorem
5.7.14
, which combines an
ordinary
2
