\operatorname{ad}/\mathfrak{z}
are isogenous,
the quantities

h^{i}(F_{S}^{+},W_{m})^{\prime}-h^{i}(F_{S}^{+},W^{0}_{m})
are bounded independently of
m

(and similarly for
h^{i}(F_{v},W_{m})^{\prime}-h^{i}(F_{v},W^{0}_{m})
, and for the
various Selmer groups introduced below).

We define Selmer groups

H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W_{m})
by

H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{m})^{\prime}\to\prod_{v\in T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m})^{\prime}\right).

We write

h_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})
for the length of
H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})
. In
the same way we define

H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W)^{\prime}\to\prod_{v\in T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W)^{\prime}\right),

H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E}):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{E})^{\prime}\to\prod_{v\in T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W_{E})^{\prime}\right),

H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}})^{\prime}\to\prod_{v\in T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}})^{\prime}\right).

Note that if we
take direct limits via the
injections
W_{m}\cong\varpi W_{m+1}\subset W_{m+1}
we obtain

H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}})=\varinjlim_{m}H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W_{m}),

while the Mittag-Leffler property means that if we take inverse limits
with respect to the projection maps
W_{m+1}\to W_{m}
we have

H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W)=\varprojlim_{m}H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W_{m})

and
thus

H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W_{E})=\left(\varprojlim_{m}H^{1}_{{\mathcal{S}},T}(F^{+},W_{m})\right)\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}E.

Lemma 7.1.7
.

Assume that
S_{p}\subseteq T
. For each
m\geq 1
, the
length of the

{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-module

\mathfrak{q}_{S,T}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S,T}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{T}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{T},\varpi^{m})

(7.1.8)

is

h_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})+\sum_{v\in T}h^{0}(G_{F^{+}_{v}},W_{m})-h^{0}(F^{+}_{S},W_{m}).

Proof.

The length of (
7.1.8
) is equal to the
length of its
{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-dual, which equals

\Hom_{{\mathcal{O}}}(\mathfrak{q}_{S,T}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S,T}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{T}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}}^{T}),{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}),

and elements of this latter group
correspond to strict equivalence classes of
T
-framed liftings
(\rho^{\prime},\{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}\}_{v\in T})
of type
{\mathcal{S}}

of
\overline{\rho}
to the ring

{\mathcal{O}}\oplus\epsilon{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
, which furthermore satisfy:

(1)

\rho^{\prime}\pmod{\epsilon}
is strictly equivalent to
(\rho,\{\gamma_{v}\}_{v\in T})
, and

(2)

for each
v\in T
,
(\gamma^{\prime}_{v})^{-1}(\rho^{\prime}|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}})\gamma^{\prime}_{v}=\gamma_{v}^{-1}(\rho|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}})\gamma_{v}
.

(Recall that since we have assumed that
S_{p}\subseteq T
, we are
not imposing any conditions at the places
v\in S\smallsetminus T
.)
Suppose that
[\phi]\in H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})
, and let
\phi
be a
lift of
[\phi]
to
Z^{1}(F_{S}^{+},W_{m})
. By definition, for each

v\in T
we can write
\phi|_{G_{F_{v}^{+}}}=d\psi_{v}
for
some
\psi_{v}\in Z^{0}(F^{+}_{v},W_{m})=W_{m}
. Then

((1+\epsilon\phi)\rho,\{(1-\epsilon\psi_{v})\gamma_{v}\}_{v\in T})

defines a
T
-framed lifting of
\overline{\rho}
, and we claim that the
strict equivalence classes satisfying the two conditions above are
exactly those containing

((1+\epsilon\phi)\rho,\{(1+\epsilon(a_{v}-\psi_{v}))\gamma_{v}\}_{v\in T})
for some
[\phi]\in H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})
and

a_{v}\in H^{0}(G_{F^{+}_{v}},W_{m})
. (Implicit in the claim is that this set
of strict equivalence classes does not depend on the choices of
liftings
\phi

and elements
\psi_{v}
.) Given this claim, the
lemma follows immediately (because the only strict equivalences
between such
T
-framed liftings are given by replacing all
the
a_{v}
with
a_{v}+a
for some
a\in H^{0}(F_{S}^{+},W_{m})
).

To establish the claim, we firstly check that

((1+\epsilon\phi)\rho,\{(1-\epsilon\psi_{v})\gamma_{v}\}_{v\in T})
is a lift of the
required form. Condition (
1
) is obvious, while (
2
) is
equivalent to asking that
\phi|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}}(g)=\psi_{v}-g\psi_{v}
for all
v\in T
,
g\in G_{F^{+}_{v}}
, which is true by the choice of
the
\psi_{v}
.

Conversely, if
(\rho^{\prime},\{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}\}_{v\in T})
satisfies (
1
),
then after replacing
\rho^{\prime}
by a strictly equivalent
representation, we can and do assume that

(\rho^{\prime},\{\gamma^{\prime}_{v}\}_{v\in T})\pmod{\epsilon}=(\rho,\{\gamma_{v}\}_{v\in T})
. We may write

\rho^{\prime}=(1+\epsilon\phi)\rho
for some
\phi\in Z^{1}(F_{S}^{+},W_{m})^{\prime}
, and
write
\gamma^{\prime}_{v}=(1+\epsilon a_{v})\gamma_{v}
with
a_{v}\in W_{m}
. Then (
2
)
is equivalent to
asking that for each
v\in T
and
we have
\phi|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}}=-da_{v}
. Thus we require that
[\phi]\in H_{{\mathcal{S}},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})
, and given this, the possible
a_{v}
differ by
elements of
H^{0}(G_{F^{+}_{v}},W_{m})
, as required.
∎

We define dual Selmer groups as follows. We let
W^{0*}
be the

{\mathcal{O}}
-module dual of
W^{0}
, so that
W^{0*}_{m}:=W^{0*}/\varpi^{m}
is the

{\mathcal{O}}/\varpi^{m}
-module dual of
W_{m}
(and similarly
W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}:=W^{0*}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}E/{\mathcal{O}}\simeq\Hom(W^{0},E/{\mathcal{O}})
).
Then we set

H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},T}^{1}(F^{+},W^{0*}_{m}(1)):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W^{0*}_{m}(1))\to\prod_{v\in S\smallsetminus T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W^{0*}_{m}(1))\right),

and similarly

H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},T}^{1}(F^{+},W^{0*}(1)):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W^{0*}(1))\to\prod_{v\in S\smallsetminus T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W^{0*}(1))\right),

H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E}^{0*}(1)):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{E}^{0*}(1))\to\prod_{v\in S\smallsetminus T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W_{E}^{0*}(1))\right),

H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},T}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1)):=\ker\left(H^{i}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))\to\prod_{v\in S\smallsetminus T}H^{i}(F_{v}^{+},W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1))\right).

Just as for the
Selmer groups, these satisfy the obvious compatibilities with direct and
inverse limits.

We now show the existence of appropriate sets of Taylor–Wiles primes. We closely follow
the proofs of
[
NT23
, Cor. 2.21, Lem. 2.26, Cor. 2.27]
,
beginning with the following lemma.

Lemma 7.1.9
.

Suppose
that
\rho:G_{F^{+}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathcal{O}})
satisfies Hypothesis
7.1.3
.
Let

q:=\operatorname{corank}_{{\mathcal{O}}}H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
.

Then there exist

\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{q}\in G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})}
such
that

(a)

for each
i=1,\dots,q
,

\overline{\rho}(\sigma_{i})
is regular semi-simple, and

(b)

the kernel of the map

\displaystyle H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))

\displaystyle\to\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}H^{1}(\widehat{{\mathbf{Z}}},W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1))

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{q}W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)/(\sigma_{i}-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)

(
the product of the restriction
maps
\operatorname{Res}^{G_{F^{+},S}}_{\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle}
associated to the
homomorphisms
\widehat{{\mathbf{Z}}}\to G_{F^{+},S}
, the

i^{\text{th}}
such homomorphism sending
1
to

\sigma_{i}
)
is a finite length
{\mathcal{O}}
-module.

Proof.

Since
H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
is cofinitely
generated, it suffices (by an obvious inductive construction) to show that for any non-zero homomorphism

f:E/{\mathcal{O}}\to H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
, we can find

\sigma\in G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})}
such that
\overline{\rho}(\sigma)
is
regular semi-simple, and the restriction

\operatorname{Res}^{G_{F^{+},S}}_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\circ f:E/{\mathcal{O}}\to W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)/(\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
is non-zero.

Let
L_{\infty}^{\prime}/F^{+}
be the
extension cut out by
W^{0*}_{E}(1)
, and let
L_{\infty}=L_{\infty}^{\prime}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})
. We claim that
H^{1}(L_{\infty}/F^{+},W^{0*}_{E}(1))=0
. To see this, note that the extension cut out by
W^{*}_{E}(1)=W^{0*}_{E}(1)\oplus E(1)
is
L_{\infty}
, and since
W^{*}_{E}(1)
is pure, it follows from
[
Kis04
, Lemma
6.2]
that
H^{1}(L_{\infty}/F^{+},W^{*}_{E}(1))=0
, and thus
H^{1}(L_{\infty}/F^{+},W^{0*}_{E}(1))=0
as claimed.
Thus
H^{1}(L_{\infty}/F^{+},W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1))
is killed by a power of

p
(since it injects into the finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}
-module
H^{2}(L_{\infty}/F^{+},W^{0*}_{{\mathcal{O}}}(1))
),
and hence the homomorphism

\displaystyle\operatorname{Res}^{G_{F^{+},S}}_{G_{L_{\infty},S_{L_{\infty}}}}\circ f:E/{\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\to H^{1}(F_{S}/L_{\infty},W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1))^{G_{F^{+},S}}

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{G_{F^{+},S}}(G_{L_{\infty},S_{L_{\infty}}},W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1))

is
still non-zero (here
S_{L_{\infty}}
denotes the set of places
of
L_{\infty}
lying over places in
S
).

Let
M\subset W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
be the
{\mathcal{O}}
-submodule
generated by the elements
f(x)(\sigma)
,
x\in E/{\mathcal{O}}
,
\sigma\in G_{L_{\infty}}
; it is a non-zero divisible

{\mathcal{O}}[G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})}]
-submodule of

W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
. By the assumption
that
\rho(G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})})
is integrally enormous, we deduce
that there
exists
\sigma\in G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})}
such that

\overline{\rho}(\sigma)
is regular semi-simple and
M\not\subset(\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
. Consequently,
there exists
m\geq 0
and
\tau\in G_{L_{\infty}}
such that
f(1/\varpi^{m})(\tau)\not\in(\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
.

If
f(1/\varpi^{m})(\sigma)\not\in(\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
,
then
\operatorname{Res}^{G_{F^{+},S}}_{\langle\sigma\rangle}\circ f
is non-zero,
as required. On the other hand if

f(1/\varpi^{m})(\sigma)\in(\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
then

\operatorname{Res}^{G_{F^{+},S}}_{\langle\tau\sigma\rangle}\circ f
is non-zero
(because
f(1/\varpi^{m})(\tau\sigma)=f(1/\varpi^{m})(\tau)+f(1/\varpi^{m})(\sigma)
and
(\tau\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)=(\sigma-1)W^{0*}_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}(1)
).
By construction,

\tau\in G_{L_{\infty}}\subset G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})}

so
\tau\sigma\in G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{\infty}})}
.
Finally, since
\tau
lies in
G_{L_{\infty}}
,
\overline{\rho}(\tau)
is scalar and hence
\overline{\rho}(\tau\sigma)

is regular semi-simple,
so we are done.
∎

Definition 7.1.10
.

A
set of Taylor–Wiles primes of
level
N
is a finite set of finite places
Q
of
F^{+}
, disjoint from
S
,
such that for each
v\in Q
, we have
q_{v}\equiv 1\bmod p^{N}
,
and
\overline{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{v})
is regular semi-simple.

Given a set of Taylor–Wiles primes

Q
, we define the
augmented global deformation problem

{\mathcal{S}}_{Q}=(\overline{\rho},S\cup Q,\{\Lambda_{v}\}_{v\in S_{p}}\cup\{{\mathcal{O}}\}_{v\in(S\cup Q)\smallsetminus S_{p}},\{{\mathcal{D}}_{v}^{\triangle}\}_{v\in S_{p}}\cup\{{\mathcal{D}}_{v}^{\square}\}_{v\in(S\cup Q)\smallsetminus S_{p}}).

Lemma 7.1.11
.

Suppose
that
\rho:G_{F^{+}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathcal{O}})
satisfies Hypothesis
7.1.3
.
Let

q:=\operatorname{corank}_{{\mathcal{O}}}H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
.

Then there exist constants
d_{1},d_{2}\geq 0
such that for each
N\geq 1
we
can find a set of Taylor–Wiles primes

Q_{N}
of level

N
such that

(1)

\#Q_{N}=q
.

(2)

h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))\leq d_{1}
.

(3)

for each
m\leq N
, the length of

\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{S}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S},\varpi^{m})

is at most

d_{2}+m\bigl(2q-4[F^{+}:{\mathbf{Q}}]+\#S-1\bigr)
.

Proof.

Let
Q
be any set of Taylor–Wiles primes.
We have by definition
the exact sequence

0\to H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))\to H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))\to\oplus_{v\in Q}H^{1}(k(v),W_{N}^{0*}(1)).

More generally, for a set
\mathcal{Q}
of elements
\sigma_{i}\in G_{F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{N}})}

with
\overline{\rho}(\sigma_{i})

semi-simple,
let us denote
by
H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))

the kernel of the map

H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))\to\oplus_{\mathcal{Q}}H^{1}(\langle\sigma_{i}\rangle,W_{N}^{0*}(1)).

Accordingly,
if
Q=\{v_{i}\}
is a set of Taylor–Wiles primes,
and
\mathcal{Q}=\{\sigma_{i}\}
with
\sigma_{i}=\mathrm{Frob}_{v_{i}}
, then

H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))=H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1)).

Moreover, by the Chebotarev density theorem, for any
such set
\mathcal{Q}=\{\sigma_{i}\}
, there exists a set of
places
v_{i}
with
q_{v_{i}}\equiv 1\bmod p^{N}

such that the action of
\mathrm{Frob}_{v_{i}}
on the finite
module
W_{N}^{0*}(1)
coincides with the action
of
\sigma_{i}
, and such that for any
m\leq N
and any
f\in H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))
, we have

f(\sigma_{i})=f(\mathrm{Frob}_{v_{i}})
. (Here we use that the

{\mathcal{O}}
-modules
H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))
are finitely
generated and the modules
W_{m}^{0*}(1)
are finite.) This implies the equality

H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))=H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1)).

(7.1.12)

for any
m\leq N
.
Comparing to Lemma
7.1.9
, we see that
we can and do choose a set of elements
\mathcal{Q}=\{\sigma_{i}\}
so that the groups

H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
are finite length

{\mathcal{O}}
-modules of uniformly bounded length (indeed they are all
isomorphic, but we do not need this),
and let
Q_{N}
denote a corresponding set of Taylor–Wiles primes of level
N
so
that equality
7.1.12
holds. We will now show that these
sets in fact satisfy properties (
2
) and (
3
).

Considering the long exact sequences in Galois cohomology
associated to the short exact sequence

0\to W_{N}^{0*}(1)\to W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varpi^{N}}}{{\to}}W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1)\to 0

we see that we have a
morphism

H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))=H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))\to H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))[\varpi^{N}]

whose
kernel has order bounded by that of
H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))/\varpi^{N}
. Since
as explained above

h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
is uniformly
bounded, in order to prove (
2
), it remains to show that
H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
has
finite length. It in turn suffices to check that

H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E}^{0*}(1))=0
(because then

H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
is cofinitely generated and injects
into the finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}
-module
H^{1}(F^{+},W^{0*}(1))
). But

H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E}^{0*}(1))=H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E}^{0}(1))
, and we even have

H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E}(1))=0
, because
\rho
is absolutely irreducible and
not isomorphic to
\rho(1)
(e.g. because it has different
Hodge–Tate weights).

We now turn to (
3
). Write
l
for the length of a finite
{\mathcal{O}}
-module. By
the Greenberg–Wiles formula together
with Remark
7.1.6
, the
quantity

h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m})-h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))+h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))-h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0})\\
+\sum_{v\in S}h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})+\sum_{v\in Q_{N}}\left(h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})-h^{1}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})\right)+\sum_{v|\infty}l((1+c_{v})W_{m}^{0})

is uniformly bounded independently of
N
and
m\leq N
(and of our choice
of
Q_{N}
).

Comparing to Lemma
7.1.7
, we see that in order to
establish (
3
), it
suffices to show that the quantity

-h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))+h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))\\
+\biggl(h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m})-h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0})-m\biggr)+\sum_{v\in S}\biggl(h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})-h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m})+m\biggr)\\
+\sum_{v\in Q_{N}}\biggl(h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})-h^{1}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})+2m\biggr)+\sum_{v|\infty}\biggl(l\bigl((1+c_{v}\bigr)W_{m}^{0})-4m\biggr)

is uniformly bounded independently of
N
and of
m\leq N
. We will do
this by showing that each of the terms is uniformly bounded.

We begin with

h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))
. Considering the
morphisms

W_{m}^{0*}(1)\to W_{N}^{0*}(1)\to W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1)

we
obtain a morphism

H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))=H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))\to H_{{\mathcal{S}}_{\mathcal{Q}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))

whose kernel is
contained in the kernel of the
morphism

H^{1}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))\to H^{1}(F^{+}_{S}/F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1)).

This latter kernel is isomorphic
to a
subquotient of
H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
, which we showed above is
a finite
{\mathcal{O}}
-module. The uniform boundedness of

h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))
for
m\leq N
then follows from that of

h_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{N}^{0*}(1))
, i.e. from
(
2
).

To show that the term
h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))
is uniformly bounded,
we recall from above that
H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E}^{0*}(1))=0
. It follows that we
have an injective map:

H^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))\hookrightarrow H^{1}(F^{+},W^{0*})[\varpi^{m}],

and we are done
because
H^{1}(F^{+},W^{0*})[\varpi^{m}]
is uniformly bounded (since

H^{1}(F^{+},W^{0*})
is a finitely generated
{\mathcal{O}}
-module). The
term

h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m})-h^{0}(F^{+},W_{m}^{0})-m

and the terms for
v\in S
can be handled similarly, using
that
H^{0}(F^{+},W_{E})=H^{0}(F^{+},W^{0}_{E})\oplus E
(respectively

H^{0}(F^{+}_{v},W_{E})=H^{0}(F^{+}_{v},W^{0}_{E})\oplus E
).

If
v\in Q_{N}
, then since
v
splits in
F^{+}(\zeta_{p^{N}})
and
m\leq N
, the local
Euler characteristic formula and Tate local duality
give

h^{1}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})-h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})=h^{2}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0})=h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0*}(1))=h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0*}),

and
since
\overline{\rho}({\mathrm{Frob}_{v}})
is regular semi-simple, we have

h^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{m}^{0*})=2m
, so these terms vanish identically.

Finally the claim for places
v|\infty
follows easily from

\dim_{E}H^{0}(F_{v}^{+},W_{E}^{0})=4
(which in turn follows from the assumption
that
\nu\circ\rho=\varepsilon^{-1}
).
∎

Proposition 7.1.13
.

Suppose that
\rho:G_{F^{+}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathcal{O}})
satisfies
Hypothesis
7.1.3
. Let

q:=\operatorname{corank}_{{\mathcal{O}}}H_{{\mathcal{S}}^{\perp},S}^{1}(F^{+},W_{E/{\mathcal{O}}}^{0*}(1))
.

Then there exists an integer
d\geq 0
such that for each
N\geq 1
we
can find a set of Taylor–Wiles primes

Q_{N}
of level

N
, together with a morphism in
\operatorname{CNL}_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}

R_{{\mathcal{S}},S}^{\operatorname{loc}}\llbracket X_{1},\ldots,X_{g}\rrbracket\rightarrow R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S},

(7.1.14)

such that:

(1)

\#Q_{N}=q
.

(2)

g=2q-4[F^{+}:{\mathbf{Q}}]+\#S-1
.

(3)

Let
\mathfrak{q}_{S,g}^{\operatorname{loc}}
be the kernel of the composite morphism

R_{{\mathcal{S}},S}^{\operatorname{loc}}\llbracket X_{1},\ldots,X_{g}\rrbracket\rightarrow R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S}\to{\mathcal{O}}

determined by
\rho
and (
7.1.14
).
Then the finite
{\mathcal{O}}
-module

\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{S,g}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S})

is killed by
\varpi^{d}
.

Proof.

Choose the Taylor–Wiles primes
Q_{N}
as in Lemma
7.1.11
, and let
d
be the constant
d_{2}
in the
statement of that lemma. We can without loss of generality assume
that
N>d
(because a set of Taylor–Wiles primes of level
N+1
is
also a set of Taylor–Wiles primes of level
N
).
If
M=\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{S}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S})
,
the length of
M/\varpi^{m}

is at most
gm+d
for all
m\leq N
by
Lemma
7.1.11
(
3
).
Thus,
by
[
NT23
, Lem. 2.20]
,
we can
find a map

{\mathcal{O}}^{g}\to M/\varpi^{N}=\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{S}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S},\varpi^{N})

whose cokernel has length at
most
d
, and is in particular killed
by
\varpi^{d}
. By the topological
version of Nakayama’s lemma, we can find a presentation (
7.1.14
) such that

\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{S,g}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S},\varpi^{N})

is killed by
\varpi^{d}
. Since we are
assuming that
N>d
, this implies that

\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}/(\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{S,g}^{\operatorname{loc}}\cdot R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S})

is killed by
\varpi^{d}
, as required.
∎

7.2.
An abstract patching argument

Set
\Delta_{\infty}:={\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{2q}
.
Suppose that we have the following data.

Hypothesis 7.2.1
.

(1)

An
R_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-module
M
, which is finite free as a

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
-module.

(2)

For each integer
N\geq 1
, a finite quotient
\Delta_{N}

of
\Delta_{\infty}
, such that

\Delta_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta_{\infty}/p^{N}
factors through
\Delta_{N}
.

(3)

For each
N\geq 1
, a homomorphism
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}[\Delta_{N}]\to R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
, and a finite

R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
-module
M_{N}
which is finite free as a
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}[\Delta_{N}]
-module.

(4)

Isomorphisms of
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
-modules

M_{N}\otimes_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}[\Delta_{N}]}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}M,

(where the homomorphism

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}[\Delta_{N}]\to\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
is the
augmentation map), compatible with the actions of
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}

and
R_{{\mathcal{S}}}
and the natural homomorphism
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}\to R_{{\mathcal{S}}}
.

As in Definition
6.1.4
, we let
{\mathcal{T}}=\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}\llbracket x_{1},\dots,x_{11\#S-1}\rrbracket
be the coordinate ring of

(\prod_{v\in T}\widehat{\GSp}_{4})/\widehat{\mathbf{G}_{m}}
over

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
.
Let

S_{\infty}:=\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}\llbracket\Delta_{\infty}\rrbracket\widehat{\otimes}_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}{\mathcal{T}},

and let
\mathbf{a}_{\infty}
be the kernel of the map

S_{\infty}\to\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
given by sending each element
of
\Delta_{\infty}
to
1
and each
x_{i}
to
0
. Write

S_{N}:=\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}[\Delta_{N}]\widehat{\otimes}_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}[\Delta_{N}]
,
a quotient of
S_{\infty}
. Let

R_{\infty}:=R_{{\mathcal{S}},S}^{\operatorname{loc}}\llbracket X_{1},\ldots,X_{g}\rrbracket.

For each
N\geq 1
we write
R_{N}:=R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}^{S}
and
\mathfrak{q}_{N}=\mathfrak{q}_{S\cup Q_{N},S}
, so that
R_{N}
is an
R_{\infty}
-algebra
via (
7.1.14
) and an
S_{N}
-algebra via (
6.1.5
) and Hypothesis
7.2.1
(
3
). We
set

M_{N}^{\square}:=M_{N}\otimes_{R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}}R_{N}=M_{N}\otimes_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}{\mathcal{T}},

where the equality follows from (
6.1.5
). Write
R=R_{{\mathcal{S}}}
, which is naturally an

R_{N}
-algebra for each
N
.

Then Hypothesis
7.2.1

implies that:

•

The
R
-module
M
is finite free as a

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
-module.

•

For each
N\geq 1
, we have a homomorphism
S_{N}\to R_{N}
,
and
M^{\square}_{N}
is an
R_{N}
-module
which is finite free as an
S_{N}
-module.

•

We have isomorphisms of
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
-modules

M_{N}^{\square}/\mathbf{a}_{\infty}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}M,

compatible with the actions of
R_{N}

and
R
and the homomorphism
R_{N}\to R
.

Fix a non-principal ultrafilter
{\mathcal{F}}
on
\mathbf{N}
, and
write
\mathbf{\Lambda}:=\prod_{N\in\mathbf{N}}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
, and write

\mathbf{\Lambda}_{x}
for the localization of
\mathbf{\Lambda}

at the prime ideal

x:=\{(x_{N})_{N\in\mathbf{N}}|\exists I\in{\mathcal{F}}\textrm{
s.t. }\forall N\in I,x_{N}\in\mathfrak{m}_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}\}.

Then we
set

M_{\infty}:=\varprojlim_{n}\biggl(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{x}\otimes_{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\prod_{N\gg 0}M_{N}^{\square}/\mathbf{a}_{\infty}^{n}\biggr),

where the product is over the
cofinite (by Hypothesis
7.2.1
(
2
)) set of
N

for which
\mathbf{a}_{\infty}^{n}\supseteq\ker(S_{\infty}\to S_{N})
, and we set

R^{\textrm{patch}}:=\varprojlim_{n}\biggl(\mathbf{\Lambda}_{x}\otimes_{\mathbf{\Lambda}}\prod_{N\geq 1}R_{N}/\mathfrak{m}_{R_{N}}^{n}\biggr).

By for example
[
GN20
, Prop. 3.4.16]
, we
have in particular produced the following structures.

•

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
-algebra homomorphisms

S_{\infty}\to R_{\infty}\to R^{\textrm{patch}}\twoheadrightarrow R
.

•

A finite free
S_{\infty}
-module
M_{\infty}
, together with an
isomorphism of
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}
-modules

M_{\infty}\otimes_{S_{\infty}}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}M.

•

A commutative diagram of

S_{\infty}
-algebras

\textstyle{R_{\infty}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\End_{S_{\infty}}(M_{\infty})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\otimes_{S_{\infty}}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\End_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},F^{+}}}(M)}

Write
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}}\subset R^{\mathrm{patch}}
and

q_{\infty}\subset R_{\infty}
for the inverse images of
\mathfrak{q}\subset R

(i.e. the kernels of the composite morphisms

R_{\infty}\to R^{\textrm{patch}}\twoheadrightarrow R\to{\mathcal{O}}
corresponding
to
\rho
).

Lemma 7.2.2
.

The
{\mathcal{O}}
-module

\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}}/((\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}})^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{\infty})
is killed
by
\varpi^{d}
, where
d
is as in Proposition
7.1.13
.

Proof.

This is proved in exactly the same way as
[
NT23
, Prop. 4.18]
. By Proposition
7.1.13
, for each
N\geq 1
the

{\mathcal{O}}
-module
\mathfrak{q}_{N}/((\mathfrak{q}_{N})^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{\infty})
is killed by
\varpi^{d}
, so
the cokernel of

\prod_{N\geq 1}\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}/(\mathfrak{q}_{\infty})^{2}\to\prod_{N\geq 1}\mathfrak{q}_{N}/(\mathfrak{q}_{N})^{2}

is killed by
\varpi^{d}
(here the map

\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}\to\mathfrak{q}_{N}
is the one induced by the morphism

R_{\infty}\to R_{N}
). By an identical
argument to the proof of
[
NT23
, Lem. 4.16, 4.17]
, the image of

\prod_{N\geq 1}\mathfrak{q}_{N}
(resp.
\prod_{N\geq 1}\mathfrak{q}_{N}^{2}
) in
R^{\mathrm{patch}}

is
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}}
(resp.
(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}})^{2}
).

It remains to show that the image of

\prod_{N\geq 1}\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}/(\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}^{2})
in

\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}}/(\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}})^{2}
agrees with the image
of
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}/(\mathfrak{q}_{\infty})^{2}
. This follows from
[
NT23
, Lem. 4.19]
, exactly as in the proof of
[
NT23
, Prop. 4.18]
.
∎

Our abstract freeness result is the following, where for the
convenience of the reader we have recalled the running hypotheses and
notation in the statement.

Proposition 7.2.3
.

Assume that
\rho
satisfies Hypothesis
7.1.3
, and that
M
satisfies
Hypothesis
7.2.1
. Write
\mathfrak{q}
for the kernel of the
homomorphism
R_{{\mathcal{S}}}\to{\mathcal{O}}
corresponding to
\rho
. Then
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a finite free

(R_{{\mathcal{S}}})_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.2.2
(
3
) and our assumption that
\rho|_{G_{F_{v}^{+}}}
is ordinary, pure and
p
-distinguished for all
v|p
, together with
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 7.1.3]
and the assumption that
\rho
is pure, the
local ring
(R_{\infty})_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
is regular. Write

(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
for its
\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}
-adic
completion, and similarly
(R^{\operatorname{patch}})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}^{\operatorname{patch}}}
for
the
\mathfrak{q}^{\operatorname{patch}}
-adic completion of
(R^{\operatorname{patch}})_{\mathfrak{q}^{\operatorname{patch}}}
. Then
the morphism

(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}\to(R^{\operatorname{patch}})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}^{\operatorname{patch}}}

is surjective, because by
Lemma
7.2.2
the relative cotangent
space
\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}}/((\mathfrak{q}^{\mathrm{patch}})^{2},\mathfrak{q}_{\infty})

vanishes (note that localization at
\mathfrak{q}^{\operatorname{patch}}
in particular
inverts
p
).

Accordingly we can and do lift the morphism

S_{\infty}\to(R^{\operatorname{patch}})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}^{\operatorname{patch}}}
to a morphism

S_{\infty}\to(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
. Since
M_{\infty}
is a
finite free
S_{\infty}
-module, we see that the

(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
-module

(M_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
has depth

\dim S_{\infty}-1
. By the definition of
g
together with Lemma
6.2.2
(
3
)and
[
BG19
, Thm. 3.3.3]
, we have
\dim(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}=\dim S_{\infty}-1
. Since

(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
is regular, it follows from
Auslander–Buchsbaum that
(M_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
is a
finite free
(R_{\infty})^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}_{\infty}}
-module. Quotienting
by
\mathbf{a}_{\infty}
, we conclude that
M^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a finite free

R^{\wedge}_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module, and equivalently that
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a finite free

R_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module, as required.
∎

7.3.
Higher Hida theory

We now specialize to the case
F^{+}={\mathbf{Q}}
(but still allow
p\geq 2
to
be arbitrary), and continue to assume that
\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathcal{O}})

satisfies Hypothesis
7.1.3
. In
particular since
\rho|_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}
is ordinary, semistable of weight
2
, and

p
-distinguished, and we have chosen a
p
-stabilization, we have
determined an ordered pair
(\alpha_{p},\beta_{p})
of distinct elements of
{\mathcal{O}}

such that

\rho\cong\begin{pmatrix}\lambda_{\alpha_{p}}&0&*&*\\
0&\lambda_{\beta_{p}}&*&*\\
0&0&\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda_{\beta_{p}}^{-1}&0\\
0&0&0&\varepsilon^{-1}\lambda_{\alpha_{p}}^{-1}\end{pmatrix}

where
\lambda_{x}
is the unramified character with
\lambda_{x}(\mathrm{Frob}_{p})=x
.

Our next goal is to explain the construction of the data as in
Hypothesis
7.2.1

which we will use to prove our multiplicity one theorems. This amounts
to constructing Taylor–Wiles systems out of higher Hida theory modules, which we
already did for usual (i.e.
H^{0}
) Hida modules in
[
BCGP21
, §7.8, 7.9]
, and
we will follow the account there where possible. We do make some changes to the setup however: we specialize to the case
F^{+}={\mathbf{Q}}
, allow
p=2
, work
with different level structures at primes where
\rho
ramifies, and use a different argument to ensure that we can work at neat level.

We begin by recalling some of the main results of
[
BP23
]
,
specialized to the case of
\GSp_{4}
. For each
w\in{}^{M}W
, and neat tame level

K^{p}
, in
[
BP23
, §1.4, 5.4]
, we have defined perfect complexes
of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket
-modules, which we denote by
M^{\bullet}_{w,\mathrm{cusp}}

and
M^{\bullet}_{w}
. By
[
BP23
, Prop. 5.6.3]
the complexes

M^{\bullet}_{w,\mathrm{cusp}}
have amplitude in the range
[0,\ell(w)]
, and the complexes

M^{\bullet}_{w}
have amplitude in the range
[\ell(w),3]
. These
complexes have an action of
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
(extending the
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
-action) and an
action of the Hecke algebra
\mathbf{T}^{p}
of prime to
p

level. We now set

M_{w}:=\begin{cases}H^{\ell(w)}(M^{\bullet}_{w,\mathrm{cusp}})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}{\mathcal{O}},&\text{if }\ell(w)=0,1,\\[5.0pt]
H^{\ell(w)}(M^{\bullet}_{w})\otimes_{{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}}{\mathcal{O}},&\text{if }\ell(w)=2,3.\end{cases}

When we want to stress the dependence on the
tame level
K^{p}
, we write

M^{\bullet}_{w,\mathrm{cusp},K^{p}}
,
M^{\bullet}_{w,K^{p}}
,
and
M_{w,K^{p}}
-respectively.
We now summarize the main properties of these modules, writing

\mathrm{Iw}(p)\subset\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
for the Iwahori subgroup.

Theorem 7.3.1
.

The following properties hold:

(1)

The modules
M_{w}
are finite projective
{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket
-modules.

(2)

For any dominant algebraic character
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)^{+}
, let

\kappa=-w_{0,M}w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho.

There are Hecke equivariant isomorphisms

M_{w}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket,\lambda}E(-\lambda)=\begin{cases}H^{\ell(w)}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Iw}(p)K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{\kappa}(-D))^{\ord},&\text{if }\ell(w)=0,1,\\[5.0pt]
H^{\ell(w)}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Iw}(p)K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{\kappa})^{\ord},&\text{if }\ell(w)=2,3.\end{cases}

(3)

For any algebraic character
\lambda\in X^{*}(T)
with

\kappa=-w_{0,M}w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho\in X^{*}(T)^{M,+}

there are Hecke equivariant isomorphisms

M_{w}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket,\lambda}E(-\lambda)=\begin{cases}H_{w}^{\ell(w)}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}}(-D))^{\ord,T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})},&\text{if }\ell(w)=0,1,\\[5.0pt]
H_{w}^{\ell(w)}(\mathrm{Sh}_{K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{\kappa,\mathrm{sm}})^{\ord,T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})},&\text{if }\ell(w)=2,3.\end{cases}

(4)

We have a perfect duality pairing:

M_{w}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}M_{w_{0,M}ww_{0}}\rightarrow{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket

interpolating the classical Serre duality.

(5)

If we have a normal subgroup

K_{1}^{p}\subseteq K_{2}^{p}
then
M_{w,K_{1}^{p}}
is a finite projective
{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket[K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}]
-module and the pullback and trace induce isomorphisms

M_{w,K_{2}^{p}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{w,K_{1}^{p}}^{K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}}\qquad(M_{w,K_{1}^{p}})_{K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{w,K_{2}^{p}}.

Proof.

By duality (
[
BP23
, Thm 5.5.2]
), point (1) follows from the vanishing theorem
[
BP23
, Prop. 5.6.3]
combined with the fact that when
\ell(w)=1
the
H^{0}

with support vanishes. Point (2) is the classicality theorem
[
BP23
, Cor 4.5.5]
combined
with the control theorem
[
BP23
, Thm 5.3.5]
. Point (3) is the
comparison between higher Hida and Coleman theories
[
BP23
, Thm
6.2.9]
together with Theorem

4.6.56
. As already remarked, the duality in point (4) is
[
BP23
, Thm 5.5.2]
.

We give some justification for point (5) as this is not explained in

[
BP23
]
. The projectivity is a consequence of the other
statements and Lemma
7.3.2
below. By the vanishing results just recalled it suffices to show that the corresponding statements for the higher Hida complexes. In other words, pullback and trace induce quasi-isomorphisms

M_{w,K_{2}^{p}}^{\bullet}\to R\Gamma(K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p},M_{w,K_{1}^{p}}^{\bullet}),\quad M_{w,K_{1}^{p}}\otimes^{L}_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket[K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}]}{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket\to M_{w,K_{2}^{p}}^{\bullet},

and the same statement holds for the cuspidal complexes. For this, the key
point is to show that for a suitable choice of cone decomposition
\Sigma
, for
the integral models
\pi:\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{1}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}\to\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{2}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}
considered in
[
BP23
]
there is an
action of
K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}
on
\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{1}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}
, and the natural map

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{2},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}}\to R\Gamma(K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p},R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{1}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}})

is an isomorphism, and the analogous statements for the sheaves

\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{i},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}}(-D)
, and for traces. For this, the
facts that

R\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{1}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}}=\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{1}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}}

and

\pi_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{1}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}}^{K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{Sh}_{K_{p}K_{2}^{p},\Sigma}^{\mathrm{tor}}}

are standard and follow from the local description of the toroidal
boundary (see
[
Lan17
, Prop. 7.5]
for example). We just need to explain why
there is no higher
K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}
cohomology (note that the action of

K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}
may not be free and
p
may divide the order of

K_{2}^{p}/K_{1}^{p}
); however it again follows from the local description of the toroidal boundary that the stabilizers have order prime to
p
.
∎

We used the following (presumably standard) lemma above.

Lemma 7.3.2
.

Let
R
be a
Noetherian local ring with residue field
k
of characteristic
p
. Let
G
be
a finite group with Sylow
p
-subgroup
H
, and let
M
be a finitely
generated
R[G]
-module. Then the following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

M
is a projective
R[G]
-module.

(2)

M
is a projective (equivalently free)
R[H]
-module.

(3)

M\otimes^{L}_{R[H]}R
is a free
R
-module concentrated in degree
0
.

(4)

M\otimes^{L}_{R[H]}k
is concentrated in degree
0
.

Proof.

The equivalence of the first two conditions follows from the usual
averaging argument to promote a splitting as
R[H]
-modules to a splitting as

R[G]
-modules. Since
R[H]
is a local ring, the equivalence of the second,
third and fourth conditions is immediate from the existence of minimal free
resolutions, and in particular from
[
GN20
, Lem. 2.1.7, Prop. 2.1.9]
.
∎

Remark 7.3.3
.

Let us spell out the Hecke action at
p
. On

M_{w}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket,\lambda}E(-\lambda)
,
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
acts via
a smooth character, trivial on
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})
(this is the reason for the twist by

\lambda
). The isomorphism

M_{w}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket,\lambda}E(-\lambda)=H^{\ell(w)}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Iw}(p)K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{\kappa})^{\ord}
matches the action
of
t\in T^{+}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
with the action of the double class
[\mathrm{Iw}(p)t\mathrm{Iw}(p)]
(note
that on the left hand side, the unitary action of
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
is twisted by

-\lambda
).

Remark 7.3.4
.

Let
\kappa
be such that
\kappa=-w_{0,M}w(\lambda+\rho)-\rho
with
\lambda+\rho
being
G
-dominant. Let
c
be an eigenclass in
H^{\ell(w)}(\mathrm{Sh}_{\mathrm{Iw}(p)K^{p}}^{\mathrm{tor}},\omega^{\kappa})^{\ord}
, corresponding to an automorphic
representation
\pi
. The torus
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
acts on the Jacquet module of
\pi

via a smooth
character
\chi^{sm}_{c}
.
Assume furthermore that
\pi_{\infty}
is a discrete
series representation (which is automatic if
\lambda
is sufficiently
regular), so that we have a Galois
representation
\rho_{\pi,p}:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\rightarrow\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
associated to
\pi
(see Theorem
1.8.13
).

Then
\rho_{\pi,p}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}}
is conjugate to a
B(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
-valued
representation, which we can describe explicitly as follows
(see Remark
1.8.15
).
If
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2};w)
, the Hodge–Tate weights of
\rho_{\pi}
are given in increasing order by

\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-w}{2},\frac{2-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-w}{2},\frac{4+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-w}{2},\frac{6-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-w}{2}.

If we use the upper triangular Borel in
B({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
in
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
, and use the local class field theory map
{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}}^{\mathrm{ab}}
, the character on the diagonal is given by

t\mapsto\mathrm{diag}(t^{\frac{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+w}{2}},t^{\frac{-2+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+w}{2}},t^{\frac{-4-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+w}{2}},t^{\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+w-6}{2}}),~t\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}

while
p\in{\mathbf{Q}}_{p}^{\times}
goes to

\mathrm{diag}(\chi^{sm}_{c}((1,1,p,p))p^{\frac{-\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+w}{2}},\chi^{sm}_{c}((p,1,p,1))p^{\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}+w}{2}},

\chi^{sm}_{c}((1,p,1,p))p^{\frac{-\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+w}{2}},\chi^{sm}_{c}((p,p,1,1))p^{\frac{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+w}{2}}).

In view of Remark
7.3.4
, we now give a more Galois-theoretic parametrization of the Higher Hida theories.
Recall that in Section
6.2
we defined a complete local Noetherian
{\mathcal{O}}
-algebra
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}={\mathcal{O}}\llbracket({\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}(p))^{2}\rrbracket
. We have two maps
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}(p)\times{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}(p)
(given by the projections to each factor),
corresponding to
\chi_{1},\chi_{2}:{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}^{\times}
, and there is an associated homomorphism
\psi:{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow T(\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})
which is given by

\psi(z)=\mathrm{diag}(\chi_{1}(z),\chi_{2}(z),z^{-1}\chi_{2}^{-1}(z),z^{-1}\chi_{1}(z)^{-1}).

On the other hand, consider the universal character

\chi^{un}:T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow({\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket)^{\times}.

Using
Lemma
7.3.5
and the identification of
T
and

\widehat{T}
, we can view
\chi^{un}\rho\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}:T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow({\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket)^{\times}
as a
homomorphism

\chi^{un}\rho\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}:{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}\rightarrow T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket).

Lemma 7.3.5
.

There is a unique algebra map
f:{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket\rightarrow\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}

such that
f\circ\chi^{un}\rho\nu^{-\frac{3}{2}}=\psi
.

Proof.

Using
Lemma
7.3.6
and the
identification of
T
and
\widehat{T}
,
\psi
corresponds to a character
T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rightarrow(\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{\times}
, and the lemma follows from the universal
properties of

\chi^{un}
and of
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket
.
∎

We used the following (presumably standard) lemma above.

Lemma 7.3.6
.

If
T/{\mathbf{Z}}
is a
split torus with dual
\widehat{T}
, then for any two commutative rings
R,S
,
there is a natural bijection between group homomorphisms

T(R)\to S^{\times}

and group homomorphisms

R^{\times}\to\widehat{T}(S).

Proof.

This follows from the case
T={\mathbf{G}}_{m}
, which is obvious. More precisely,
since
X_{*}(\widehat{T})=X^{*}(T)
is a free
{\mathbf{Z}}
-module, we
have

\displaystyle\Hom(T(R),S^{\times})

\displaystyle=\Hom(X_{*}(T)\otimes R^{\times},S^{\times})

\displaystyle=\Hom(X_{*}(T),\Hom(R^{\times},S^{\times}))

\displaystyle=X^{*}(T)\otimes\Hom(R^{\times},S^{\times})

\displaystyle=X_{*}(\widehat{T})\otimes\Hom(R^{\times},S^{\times})

\displaystyle=\Hom(R^{\times},X_{*}(\widehat{T})\otimes S^{\times})

\displaystyle=\Hom(R^{\times},\widehat{T}(S)),

as required.
∎

We now consider the action of the centre of the group
\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{f})
. The action of
Z(\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{f}))
on the toroidal compactification of our Shimura varieties factors into an action of
Z({\mathbf{Q}})\backslash Z(\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{f}))=\{\pm 1\}\backslash\prod_{\ell}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
(as can be seen by considering complex uniformization). From a modular perspective,
{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
acts on the
\ell
-adic Tate module of an abelian surface by scalar multiplication. The fact that we quotient by
\{\pm 1\}
witnesses the fact that
-1
is an automorphism of any abelian surface. This action extends to an action on the
M_{w,K^{p}}
(as part of the Hecke action on these modules).

When we have a
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}
-module
M
, equipped with an action of
Z({\mathbf{Q}})\backslash Z(\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{f}))
, we let
M^{|.|^{2}}
be the submodule where the centre acts via the character
(z_{\ell})_{\ell}\mapsto z_{p}^{-2}
. More generally, if we have a finite set of primes
S
, we let
M^{|.|^{2},S}
be the submodule where the group
\prod_{\ell\notin S}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
acts via the character
(z_{\ell})_{\ell\notin S}\mapsto z_{p}^{-2}
.

Remark 7.3.7
.

This definition is motivated by Theorem
7.3.1
(
2
). Under the classicality theorem, this condition corresponds to fixing the central character of automorphic forms contributing to coherent cohomology to be
|.|^{2}
.

By abuse of language, we say that the centre acts by
|.|^{2}
on
M^{|.|^{2}}
.

On the module
M_{w,K^{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}
, the subgroup
{\mathbf{Z}}_{p}^{\times}
of
Z(\mathrm{GSp}_{4}(\mathbf{A}_{f}))
acts via
z\mapsto z^{-2}
. Therefore, fixing the central action to be
|.|^{2}
amounts to asking that the group
\prod_{\ell\neq p}{\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell}^{\times}
acts trivially.
We would therefore morally have that
(M_{w,K^{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2}}=M_{w,ZK^{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}
where
ZK^{p}=\prod_{\ell\neq p}Z({\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell})K_{\ell}
. Note however that the group
ZK^{p}
is not neat (a condition we have imposed so far on our tame level). We now explain a construction which addresses this issue.

We choose a prime
r>5
such that
r\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and
r\not\equiv 1\pmod{4}
if
p=2
. We let
\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(r)
denote the subgroup of matrices which are upper-triangular and unipotent modulo
r
. If
K^{p}
is a compact open subgroup with
K_{r}=\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(r)
, then
K^{p}
is neat by
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 7.8.3]
(applied to
v=r
).

For any finite set of primes
S
(possibly empty) not containing
p
, we let

Z^{S}K^{p}=\prod_{\ell\notin S\cup\{p\}}Z({\mathbf{Z}}_{\ell})K_{\ell}\prod_{\ell\in S}K_{\ell}
.

Lemma 7.3.8
.

Let
K^{p}_{1}\subseteq K^{p}_{2}
be compact open
subgroups,
K^{p}_{1}
normal in
K^{p}_{2}
, and the
r
-components
of
K_{1}^{p},K_{2}^{p}
both being
\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(r)
. Let
S
be a finite set
of primes, with
r\in S
, and
p\notin S
.

(1)

The module

(M_{w,K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2},S}

is a finite projective
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[Z^{S}K^{p}_{2}/Z^{S}K^{p}_{1}]
module.

(2)

The module

(M_{w,K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2}}

is a finite projective
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[ZK^{p}_{2}/ZK^{p}_{1}]
module.

Proof.

Since
r\in S
, it follows that the groups
Z^{S}K^{p}_{i}
are neat
for
i=1
,
2
. On the other hand, we have an identification

(M_{w,K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2},S}=M_{w,Z^{S}K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}},

so (
1
) follows from Theorem
7.3.1
.
Next, we can take
S=\{r\}
. Then
(M_{w,K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2}}
is obtained from
(M_{w,K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2},S}
by considering the invariants for
({\mathbf{Z}}/r{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
.
Observe that
Z^{S}K^{p}_{2}/Z^{S}K^{p}_{1}=ZK^{p}_{2}/ZK^{p}_{1}
, so

(M_{w,K^{p}_{1}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|.|^{2},S}

is a finite
projective
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[Z^{S}K^{p}_{1}/Z^{S}K^{p}_{2}]
module. Note that
({\mathbf{Z}}/r{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}
acts via

({\mathbf{Z}}/r{\mathbf{Z}})^{\times}/\{\pm 1\}
and this group has order prime
to
p
. Therefore the invariants are a direct factor, as required. ∎

7.4.
Taylor–Wiles systems

We continue to fix a continuous representation
\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathcal{O}})
satisfying Hypothesis
7.1.3
.

Definition 7.4.1
.

A
neat prime for
\overline{\rho}
is a prime
r>5
such that

•

r\neq p
,

•

r\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and
r\not\equiv 1\pmod{4}
if
p=2
.

•

\rho|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{r}}}
is unramified,

•

\overline{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{r})
is regular semi-simple,

together with a fixed ordering

(\overline{\alpha}_{r,1},\overline{\alpha}_{r,2},r\overline{\alpha}^{-1}_{r,2},r\overline{\alpha}_{r,1}^{-1})

of the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{r})
.

By Hypothesis
7.1.3
parts (
4
), (
7
), and (
8
) we can and do choose a neat prime
r
.

Definition 7.4.2
.

We let
S
be the union of
\{r\}
and the set of primes at
which
\rho
is ramified (so in particular
p\in S
, because

\varepsilon
is ramified at
p
), and choose sets of Taylor–Wiles primes
Q_{N}
as
in Proposition
7.1.13
.

For any prime
l
, we let
\mathrm{Iw}(l)
denote the subgroup of
\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})

consisting of matrices which are upper-triangular modulo
l
, and we let
\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(l)
denote the subgroup of matrices which are upper-triangular and unipotent modulo
l
.

Definition 7.4.3
.

We define an open compact subgroup
K^{p}=\prod_{l}K_{l}
of
\GSp_{4}(\mathbf{A}^{\infty,p})
as follows:

•

If
l\not\in S
, then
K_{l}=\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})
.

•

K_{r}=\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(r)
.

•

If
l\in S\smallsetminus\{r\}
, then we allow any choice of
open compact

K_{l}\subseteq\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})
.

We have compact
subgroups
K^{p}_{0}(Q_{N})
,
K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})
of
K^{p}
given by

•

If
l\not\in Q_{N}
, then
K^{p}_{0}(Q_{N})_{l}=K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})_{l}=K^{p}_{l}
.

•

If
l\in Q_{N}
, then
K^{p}_{0}(Q_{N})_{l}=\mathrm{Iw}(l)
,
K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})_{l}=\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(l)
.

These groups are neat by
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 7.8.3]
.

We let

{\mathbf{T}}^{S}=\bigotimes_{l\not\in S}{\mathcal{O}}[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/\kern-1.99997pt{/}\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})]

be the ring of spherical Hecke operators away from the bad places, and similarly we set

{\mathbf{T}}^{S\cup Q_{N}}=\bigotimes_{l\not\in S\cup Q_{N}}{\mathcal{O}}[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/\kern-1.99997pt{/}\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})].

We will also make use of some Hecke operators at Iwahori level, which
we recall from
[
BCGP21
, §2.4]
. Let
l
be a prime. Assume
that
E
is large enough to contain a square root of
l
; we fix
such a choice
l^{1/2}
. The reader can easily check that
nothing before
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 2.4.3]
makes any use of the
running assumption made there that
p\neq 2
; indeed, these results are for the most part over
E
, and
use only that it is a field of characteristic zero
containing
l^{1/2}
. We define

{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}(l)={\mathcal{O}}[G({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/\kern-1.99997pt{/}\mathrm{Iw}(l)],

{\mathcal{H}}_{1}={\mathcal{H}}_{1}(l)={\mathcal{O}}[G({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/\kern-1.99997pt{/}\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(l)].

With
T

denoting our usual maximal torus in
\GSp_{4}
, we set

T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})_{1}:=(\ker T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})\to T({\mathbf{F}}_{l})),

and exactly
as in
[
BCGP21
, Prop. 2.4.2]
we have an injective
homomorphism

T({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/(\ker T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})\to T({\mathbf{F}}_{l}))\to({\mathcal{H}}_{1})^{\times}.

(7.4.4)

The injection (
7.4.4
) induces an injective
homomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}[T({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})_{1}]\to{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
, and we identify

{\mathcal{O}}[T({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})_{1}]
with its image in
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
.

Definition 7.4.5
.

Assume that
l\equiv 1\pmod{p}
. Given elements

\overline{\alpha}_{l,1},\overline{\alpha}_{2}\in k^{\times}
, we let

\mathfrak{m}_{\overline{\alpha}_{1},\overline{\alpha}_{2}}
denote the kernel of the
homomorphism
{\mathcal{O}}[T({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})_{1}]\to k
induced by the
character
T({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})_{1}\to k^{\times}
sending

T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})\mapsto 1
,
\beta_{l,0}\mapsto 1
,

\beta_{l,1}\mapsto\overline{\alpha}_{l,1}
,

\beta_{l,2}\mapsto\overline{\alpha}_{l,1}\overline{\alpha}_{l,2}
.

We define the following elements of

T({\mathbf{Q}}_{p})
which act on
M_{w}
:

\displaystyle U_{p,1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}(1,1,p,p)

\displaystyle U_{p,2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}(p,1,p^{2},p)

\displaystyle U_{p,0}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}(p,p,p,p)

We let

\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{an}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S}
be the maximal ideal
corresponding to
\overline{\rho}
; so by definition
\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{an}}
contains
\varpi
,
and the polynomials
\det(X-\overline{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{l}))
and
Q_{l}(X)
are
congruent modulo
\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{an}}

for each
l\not\in S
.
We define

\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{an},Q_{N}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{S\cup Q_{N}}
in the same way. We let

{\mathbf{T}}={\mathbf{T}}^{S}[U_{p,0},U_{p,1},U_{p,2}]

and

{\mathbf{T}}^{Q_{N}}={\mathbf{T}}^{S\cup Q_{N}}[U_{p,0},U_{p,1},U_{p,2}],

and additionally we let
\mathfrak{m}\subset{\mathbf{T}}
be the maximal ideal

\mathfrak{m}=(\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{an}},U_{p,0}-1,U_{p,1}-\alpha_{p},U_{p,2}-\alpha_{p}\beta_{p})

and we let
\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}}\subset{\mathbf{T}}^{Q_{N}}
be the maximal ideal

\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}}=(\mathfrak{m}^{\mathrm{an},Q_{N}},U_{p,0}-1,U_{p,1}-\alpha_{p},U_{p,2}-\alpha_{p}\beta_{p}).

For each
q\in Q_{N}
, fix an ordering
\overline{\alpha}_{q,1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2}^{-1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,1}^{-1}

of the eigenvalues of
\overline{\rho}(\mathrm{Frob}_{q})
. Set

\Delta_{N}=\Delta_{Q_{N}}=\prod_{q\in Q_{N}}{\mathbf{F}}_{q}^{\times}(p)^{2}
. We now fix a choice
of
w\in{}^{M}W
, and consider the finite free
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}
-module

M:=\Hom_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}}((M_{K^{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2}},\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})_{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{r}},

(7.4.6)

and the finite
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
-modules

M_{N}:=\Hom_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}}((M_{K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2}},\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})_{\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}},\mathfrak{m}_{r},\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{N}}},

where:

•

M_{K^{p}}
and
M_{K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})}
denote the modules
M_{w}
of
Theorem
7.3.1
, taking
K^{p}
there to be
respectively our
K^{p}
and
K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})
.

•

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}
is an
{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket
-algebra via Lemma
7.3.5
.

•

The localizations

\mathfrak{m},{\mathfrak{m}}^{Q_{N}}
are
defined above.

•

The localization
\mathfrak{m}_{r}
and the localization
\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{N}}
are with respect to the
maximal ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{l}
of the subalgebras
{\mathcal{O}}[T({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})/T({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})_{1}]
of the pro-
l
Iwahori Hecke
algebras
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}(l)
for
l\in Q_{N}\cup\{r\}
as in Definition
7.4.5
.

•

The action of
\Delta_{Q_{N}}
on
M_{K^{p}_{1}(Q_{N})}
is induced by
the actions of
{\mathcal{O}}[T({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})/T({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})_{1}]
for
q\in Q_{N}
, by regarding

{\mathbf{F}}_{q}^{\times}(p)^{2}
as the maximal
p
-power quotient of

T({\mathbf{F}}_{q})/Z({\mathbf{F}}_{q})
, where
Z
denotes the centre
of
\GSp_{4}
.

•

The superscript
|\cdot|^{2}
denotes that we are fixing the central
character.

Lemma 7.4.7
.

M
is a free
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}
-module, and
M_{N}
is a free
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
-module.

Proof.

Since
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
is a local ring,
this follows from Lemma
7.3.8
(
2
),
since
\mathrm{Iw}(q)Z({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})/\mathrm{Iw}_{1}(q)Z({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})\simeq T({\mathbf{F}}_{q})/Z({\mathbf{F}}_{q})
.
∎

The following is essentially
[
GG12
, Lem. 7.1.1]
, adapted slightly
to allow
p=2
; we will use it in the proof of Proposition
7.4.10
.

Lemma 7.4.8
.

Let

\Gamma
be a profinite group, and let
S\subset R
be complete local
Noetherian rings with
\mathfrak{m}_{R}\cap S=\mathfrak{m}_{S}
and common residue
field
k
. Let
\rho:\Gamma\to\GSp_{4}(R)
be a
continuous representation. Suppose that
\rho\text{ mod }\mathfrak{m}_{R}
is
absolutely irreducible,
that
\operatorname{tr}\rho(\Gamma)\subset S
, and that

\nu\circ\rho(\Gamma)\subset S^{\times}
. Then there is a

\widehat{\GSp}_{4}(R)
-conjugate of
\rho
whose image is
contained in
\GSp_{4}(S)
.

Proof.

By
[
CHT08
, Lem. 2.1.10]
, there is some
B\in\widehat{\GL}_{4}(R)
such that

\rho^{\prime}:=B\rho B^{-1}
is valued in
\GL_{4}(S)
. Since
J^{-1}\rho J=(\nu\circ\rho)\rho^{-t}
, we have

(BJB^{t})^{-1}\rho^{\prime}(BJB^{t})=(\nu\circ\rho^{\prime})(\rho^{\prime})^{-t}.

By choosing a symplectic basis for the alternating form determined by

BJB^{t}
, it follows that
\rho^{\prime}
is
\widehat{\GSp}_{4}(S)
-conjugate to
a representation
\rho^{\prime\prime}
valued in
\GSp_{4}(S)
. By Schur’s lemma
[
CHT08
, Lem. 2.1.8]
,
we see the element in
\widehat{\GL}_{4}(R)
conjugating
\rho

to
\rho^{\prime}
is necessarily contained in
\widehat{\GSp}_{4}(R)
, as
required.
∎

Definition 7.4.9
.

Let
{\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
(resp.
{\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
) denote the image
of
{\mathbf{T}}
(resp. of
{\mathbf{T}}^{Q_{N}}
)
in
\End_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}}(M)
(resp. in

\End_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}}(M_{N}))
. (These are objects
of
\operatorname{CNL}_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}}
, but under the present hypotheses we
do not know that these algebras are nonzero (because we do not know
that
\overline{\rho}
is modular).)

Recall that we have defined the global deformation problems

{\mathcal{S}}=(\overline{\rho},S,\{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},p}\}\cup\{{\mathcal{O}}\}_{l\in S\smallsetminus\{p\}},\{{{\mathcal{D}}}_{p}^{\triangle}\}\cup\{{\mathcal{D}}_{l}^{\square}\}_{l\in S\smallsetminus\{p\}}),

{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}=(\overline{\rho},S\cup Q_{N},\{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},p}\}\cup\{{\mathcal{O}}\}_{l\in(S\cup Q_{N})\smallsetminus\{p\}},\{{{\mathcal{D}}}_{p}^{\triangle}\}\cup\{{\mathcal{D}}_{l}^{\square}\}_{l\in(S\cup Q_{N})\smallsetminus\{p\}}).

The
deformation ring
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
is a

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
-algebra via
Lemma
6.1.6
(i.e.
\Delta_{Q_{N}}
acts via the
characters
\gamma_{q,i}\circ{\operatorname{Art}}_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{q}}
).
For ease of notation, we sometimes (e.g. in the statement of the
following proposition) adopt the convention that
Q_{0}=\emptyset
, so
that for example
{\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}={\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}}
.

Proposition 7.4.10
.

For each
N\geq 0
, the
action of
\Delta_{Q_{N}}
on
M_{N}
makes
{\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
a

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
-algebra, and there is
a
\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
-algebra homomorphism

R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}\to{\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
with corresponding representation

\rho_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}})
determined by
the property that

\det(X-\rho_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}(\mathrm{Frob}_{l}))=Q_{l}(X)
for all
l\notin S\cup Q_{N}
.

Proof.

Since
M_{N}
is a finite free

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}
-module, this follows from
local-global compatibility for the Galois representations at a dense
set of points of regular weight. More precisely, it can be proved in exactly the same
way as the case
I=\emptyset
of
[
BCGP21
, Thm. 7.9.4]
, using Lemma
7.4.8
in place of
[
GG12
, Lem. 7.1.1]
. (The fact that
{\mathbf{T}}_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
is automatically a

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\Delta_{Q_{N}}]
-algebra was not recorded in

[
BCGP21
]
; it follows from local-global compatibility at the
places in
Q_{N}
.) ∎

In particular, Proposition
7.4.10

makes
M
into an
R_{{\mathcal{S}}}
-module, and each
M_{N}
into an

R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
-module. We can also regard
M
as an

R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
-module via the natural map
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}\to R_{{\mathcal{S}}}
. For each
N\geq 1
we fix a surjection

\Delta_{\infty}\twoheadrightarrow\Delta_{Q_{N}}
, and write
\Delta_{N}
for the
corresponding quotient of
\Delta_{\infty}
. The
kernel of this surjection is contained in
(p^{N}{\mathbf{Z}}_{p})^{2q}
, since each

v\in Q_{N}
satisfies
q_{v}\equiv 1\bmod p^{N}
. At this point we have
established points (
1
)–(
3
) of Hypothesis
7.2.1
, so it only remains to check (
4
), which is the content of Lemma
7.4.15
below.

Before proving it, we recall some standard facts about Iwahori Hecke
algebras that were explained in
[
BCGP21
, §2.4]
under the
unnecessary assumption that
p\neq 2
. Indeed the only place in
[
BCGP21
, §2.4]
that relies on the assumption
p\neq 2
is
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 2.4.34]
, i.e. the statement that the spherical invariants
of a module for the Iwahori Hecke algebra are a direct summand. This
is no longer valid for
p=2
, but we will avoid this problem by making
use of Lemma
7.3.8
. We do need to
make use of the (proofs of)
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 2.4.36, 2.4.37]
, but for the convenience of the reader we
will recall the necessary arguments as we use them (making it clear as
we do so that they remain valid for
p=2
).

Suppose now that
q
is a prime with
q\equiv 1\pmod{p}
, and let

{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}(q)
be the corresponding Iwahori Hecke algebra. The Bernstein
presentation of
{\mathcal{H}}
is valid for all
p
, so we can write

{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]\widetilde{\otimes}_{{\mathcal{O}}}{\mathcal{O}}[\mathrm{Iw}(q)\backslash\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})/\mathrm{Iw}(q)],

where
the twisted tensor product is
determined by the relations
[
BCGP21
, (2.4.32)]
. The centre
of
{\mathcal{H}}
is
{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]^{W}
(where as usual
W\cong D_{8}
is the Weyl group of
\GSp_{4}
),

{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]^{W}={\mathcal{O}}[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})/\kern-1.99997pt{/}\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]

given by
x\mapsto[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]x
(where we are regarding
x

as an element of
{\mathcal{H}}
); this isomorphism agrees with the usual Satake
isomorphism. (Indeed this presentation, and the compatibility with the
Satake isomorphism, are valid over any ring containing an invertible
square root of
q
; see for example
[
Vig05
]
or the very
general results of
[
Bou21
]
.)

Since we are assuming that
q=1

in
k
, we deduce exactly as in
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 2.4.33]
that
reduction modulo
\varpi
induces a natural
isomorphism

{\mathcal{H}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}k\cong k[X_{*}(T)\rtimes W].

Since we are assuming
that
q\equiv 1\pmod{p}
,
and in any case in our applications of these results in the global setting there
is a twist which makes all of the powers of
q
integral, we will ignore all powers of
q^{1/2}

from now on.

Exactly as
in
[
BCGP21
]
, we let
x_{0}
,

x_{1}
, and
x_{2}
denote the following three cocharacters:

x_{0}:t\rightarrow\operatorname{diag}(t,t,1,1),

x_{1}:t\rightarrow\operatorname{diag}(1/t,1,1,t),

x_{2}:t\rightarrow\operatorname{diag}(1,1/t,t,1).

Then
x^{2}_{0}x_{1}x_{2}
is the cocharacter
t\mapsto\operatorname{diag}(t,t,t,t)
and

{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]={\mathcal{O}}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},(x^{2}_{0}x_{1}x_{2})^{-1}]={\mathcal{O}}[x_{0},x_{1},x_{2},(x_{0}x_{1}x_{2})^{-1}].

The action of
W
preserves
(x_{0},x_{0}x_{1},x_{0}x_{2},x_{0}x_{1}x_{2})
considered as an unordered
quadruple. Recalling that we are ignoring powers of
q^{1/2}
, under
the identification of
k[X_{*}(T)]^{W}
with the spherical Hecke
algebra we have

\displaystyle Q_{q}(X)

\displaystyle=X^{4}-T_{q,1}X^{3}+(T_{q,2}+2T_{q,0})X^{2}-T_{q,0}T_{q,1}X+T_{q,0}^{2}

\displaystyle=(X-x_{0})(X-x_{0}x_{1})(X-x_{0}x_{2})(X-x_{0}x_{1}x_{2}).

Then
k[X_{*}(T)]^{W}=k[e_{1},e_{2},(e_{3}/e_{1})^{\pm 1}]
where

\sum e_{i}X^{i}=(X-x_{0})(X-x_{0}x_{1})(X-x_{0}x_{2})(X-x_{0}x_{1}x_{2}).

Now suppose that
\overline{\alpha}_{q,1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2}\in k^{\times}
are
such that

\overline{\alpha}_{q,1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2}^{-1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,1}^{-1}

are pairwise distinct, and set
\gamma_{0}:=\overline{\alpha}_{v,1}
,

\gamma_{1}:=\overline{\alpha}_{v,2}\overline{\alpha}_{v,1}^{-1}
,

\gamma_{2}:=(\overline{\alpha}_{v,1}\overline{\alpha}_{v,2})^{-1}
.

We let
\mathfrak{n}\subset{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]^{W}
be the maximal ideal
generated by
\varpi
and the
e_{i}-e_{i}(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2})
, and for
each
w\in W
we let
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
be the maximal ideal of
{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]

generated by
\varpi
and the
w\cdot x_{i}-\gamma_{i}
. By our assumption on
\overline{\alpha}_{q,1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2}
, the 8 ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
are pairwise
distinct, and exactly as in the proof of
[
BCGP21
, Lem. 2.4.36]
,
we see that
{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]_{\mathfrak{n}}
is a semi-local ring whose maximal
ideals are the
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
. In particular if
M
is an

{\mathcal{O}}[X_{*}(T)]
-module, we can
write

M_{\mathfrak{n}}=\oplus_{w\in W}M_{\mathfrak{m}_{w}}.

Lemma 7.4.11
.

Suppose that
R
is a commutative
ring, and that
G
is a finite group. If
H
is a subgroup of
G
then we write

e_{H}:=\sum_{h\in H}h\in R[G]
, and let
[G]_{H}=\sum_{g\in G/H}g\in R[G]

(
the coset representatives being chosen arbitrarily
)
. Then
if
M
is a finite projective left

R[G]
-module, we have

(1)

M^{H}=e_{H}M
.

(2)

M^{G}=[G]_{H}M^{H}
.

(3)

If
S
is any
R
-algebra, then

(M\otimes_{R}S)^{H}=M^{H}\otimes_{R}S
.

(4)

The natural maps

M^{H}\to M
and
M\to M_{H}
induce isomorphisms of

R
-modules

\Hom_{R}(M_{H},R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{R}(M,R)^{H},

\Hom_{R}(M,R)_{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{R}(M^{H},R).

Proof.

Writing
M
as a direct summand of a free
R[G]
-module, we reduce to
the case
M=R[G]
. Then the first part is an easy calculation, while
the second part follows from the first and the relation
[G]_{H}e_{H}=e_{G}
, which
is immediate from the definitions. The third part is immediate from
the first.

Turning to the final part, it is easy to see that the isomorphism

\Hom_{R}(M_{H},R)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{R}(M,R)^{H}
holds for any finite left

R[G]
-module, projective or otherwise. It remains to show that if
M
is projective then

\Hom_{R}(M,R)_{H}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Hom_{R}(M^{H},R)
. We may again assume
that
M=R[G]
, and since
R[G]
is a free
R[H]
-module, we can
furthermore assume that
H=G
. We can identify

R[G]
with
\Hom_{R}(R[G],R)
by sending
[1]
to the
map
\phi
such that
\phi\bigl(\sum_{g}x_{g}g\bigr)=x_{1}
.
Combining this with the usual identification of

R[G]_{G}
with
R
via the trace map, we see that

\Hom_{R}(M,R)_{G}
is a free
R
-module of rank one generated by the
image of
\phi
.
By part (
1
), we have
R[G]^{G}=R\cdot e_{G}
. Since
\phi(e_{G})=1
, we are
done.∎

Lemma 7.4.12
.

Suppose that
\overline{\alpha}_{q,1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2}\in k^{\times}
are
such that

\overline{\alpha}_{q,1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2},\overline{\alpha}_{q,2}^{-1},\overline{\alpha}_{q,1}^{-1}

are pairwise distinct, and define ideals
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
,

\mathfrak{n}
as above.

Let
R
be an object of
\operatorname{CNL}_{{\mathcal{O}}}
, and let
N
be an
R
-module with a smooth
action of
\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})
, with the property that if

K_{2}\trianglelefteq K_{1}
are compact open subgroups of
\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{q})
, then
N^{K_{2}}
is a finite projective
R[K_{1}/K_{2}]
-module.

Then for each
w\in W
the
projection

\operatorname{pr}_{w}:(N^{\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})})_{\mathfrak{n}}\to(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{m}_{w}}

(7.4.13)

is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Take
K_{1}=\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})
,
K_{2}=\ker\bigl(\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})\to\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{F}}_{q})\bigr)
. Set
G=K_{1}/K_{2}=\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{F}}_{q})
, and let
H=\mathrm{Iw}(q)/K_{2}=B({\mathbf{F}}_{q})<G
. Write
M=N^{K_{2}}
,
so that by assumption
M
is a finite projective
R[G]
-module, and
we have
M^{G}=N^{\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})}
and
M^{H}=N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)}
.

By Nakayama’s lemma and Lemma
7.4.11
(
3
), we
can and do assume from now on that
R=k
.
Let
[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]\in{\mathcal{H}}
be the Hecke operator which is the indicator function
on
\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})
.
By Lemma
7.4.11
(
2
) we have

N^{\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})}=[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{F}}_{q})]_{B({\mathbf{F}}_{q})}(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})=[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})](N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)}),

where the second equality follows from the very definition of the Hecke operators.
We claim that (under our assumption that
R=k
)
the Hecke operator
[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]
is an inverse to
\operatorname{pr}_{w}
.

To see this, note firstly that by the Bruhat decomposition we have

[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]=\sum_{w\in W}w

(where we are using the
identification
{\mathcal{H}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}}k=k[X_{*}(T)\rtimes W]
). We have the
decomposition

(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{n}}=\oplus_{w\in W}(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{m}_{w}},

so we can write any
x\in(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{n}}
as
x=\sum_{w\in W}x_{w}
for unique
elements
x_{w}\in(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{m}_{w}}
, and in particular if
x\in(N^{\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})})_{\mathfrak{n}}
then
\operatorname{pr}_{w}(x)=x_{w}
. By the definition of
the
\mathfrak{m}_{w}
, we see that the action of
W
on

(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{n}}
is via
w_{1}x_{w_{2}}=x_{w_{1}w_{2}}
for all

w_{1},w_{2}\in W
.

We can therefore compute that if
x\in(N^{\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})})_{\mathfrak{n}}
then

[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]\circ\operatorname{pr}_{w}(x)=[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})]x_{w}=\sum_{w^{\prime}\in W}w^{\prime}x_{w}=\sum_{w^{\prime}\in W}x_{w^{\prime}w}=x
, while if
y\in(N^{\mathrm{Iw}(q)})_{\mathfrak{m}_{w}}
then

\operatorname{pr}_{w}\circ[\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{q})](y)=\operatorname{pr}_{w}\bigl(\sum_{w^{\prime}\in W}w^{\prime}y\bigr)=y
, as required.
∎

Remark 7.4.14
.

The second half of the proof of
Lemma
7.4.12
is
essentially identical to the proof of
[
Whi22
, Prop. 5.10]

in the special case that
G=\GSp_{4}
and
P=B
. Indeed note that
while it is assumed there that
p\nmid\#W
, all that is used in the
proof is that
p\nmid\#W_{L}
, where
L
is the Levi factor
of
P
; and for
P=B
the group
W_{L}
is trivial.

We now establish Hypothesis
7.2.1
(
4
).

Lemma 7.4.15
.

The natural pullback map
M_{K^{p}}\to M_{K_{1}^{p}(Q_{N})}
induces an isomorphism
of
R_{{\mathcal{S}}_{Q_{N}}}
-modules

(M_{N})_{\Delta_{Q_{N}}}\to M
.

Proof.

First, we have that

(M_{N})_{\Delta_{Q_{N}}}=\Hom_{\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}}((M_{K^{p}_{0}(Q_{N})}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2}},\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})_{\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}},\mathfrak{m}_{r},\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{N}}}

by
part (
4
) of Lemma
7.4.11
and Lemma
7.4.7
.
We claim that the map

(M_{K^{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2}}_{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{r}}\rightarrow(M_{K^{p}_{0}(Q_{N})}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2}}_{\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}},\mathfrak{m}_{r},\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{N}}}

is an isomorphism. It suffices to see (imposing that the central character acts by
|\cdot|^{2}
) that the map

(M_{K^{p}}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2},Q_{N}\cup\{r\}}_{\mathfrak{m},\mathfrak{m}_{r}}\to(M_{K^{p}_{0}(Q_{N})}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})_{\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}},\mathfrak{m}_{r},\mathfrak{m}_{Q_{N}}}^{|\cdot|^{2},Q_{N}\cup\{r\}}

is an isomorphism. Here the subscript
(|\cdot|^{2},Q_{N}\cup\{r\})
indicates that the central character
acts by
|\cdot|^{2}
up to primes in
Q_{N}\cup\{r\}
(as in
Lemma
7.3.8
).

Let
K(Q_{N})=\prod_{l}K(Q_{N})_{l}\subset\GSp_{4}(\mathbf{A}^{\infty,p})
be defined by

\begin{cases}K^{p}(Q_{N})_{l}=K^{p}_{l}&\text{if }l\not\in Q_{N},\\
K(Q_{N})_{l}=\ker\bigl(\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Z}}_{l})\to\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{F}}_{l})\bigr)&\text{if }l\in Q_{N}\end{cases}

By Lemma
7.3.8
(
1
),
(M_{K(Q_{N})}\otimes_{{\mathcal{O}}\llbracket T({\mathbf{Z}}_{p})\rrbracket}\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}})^{|\cdot|^{2},Q_{N}\cup\{r\}}_{\mathfrak{m}^{Q_{N}},\mathfrak{m}_{r}}
is a finite projective

\Lambda_{\GSp_{4},{\mathbf{Q}}}[\prod_{q\in Q_{N}}\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{F}}_{q})]
-module. We conclude by Lemma
7.4.12
.
∎

We summarize our results so far in the following proposition; we
remind the reader that at this point we do not know that
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is
nonzero. Indeed, even if we knew that
\rho
was modular, it could be
that our choice of subgroups
K_{l}
for
l\in S
forces
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
to be
zero. In Proposition
7.5.10
we will establish sufficient conditions under which
M_{\mathfrak{q}}

is free of rank
1
.

Proposition 7.4.16
.

Assume that
\rho
satisfies Hypothesis
7.1.3
, and let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the deformation
problem (
7.1.5
)
with
S
as in Definition
7.4.2
. Write
\mathfrak{q}
for the kernel of the homomorphism

R_{{\mathcal{S}}}\to{\mathcal{O}}
corresponding to
\rho
. Define
M
as
in (
7.4.6
). Then
M_{\mathfrak{q}}
is a finite free

(R_{{\mathcal{S}}})_{\mathfrak{q}}
-module.

Proof.

By Lemmas
7.3.8
and
7.4.15
, our
construction of the modules
M_{N}
above gives the data of
Hypothesis
7.2.1
.
The result is then immediate from Proposition
7.2.3
.
∎

7.5.
Multiplicity one

Before establishing our main multiplicity one
results we begin with some background material and preliminary lemmas. We
refer to Section
1.8.22
for the relationship between cuspidal automorphic
representations
\pi
of
\GSp_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
which are of general type, and cuspidal
automorphic representations
\Pi
of
\GL_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
of symplectic type.

Assume from now on that
F={\mathbf{Q}}
, and that furthermore
\pi
has central
character
\omega_{\pi}=|\cdot|^{2}
. We now recall some consequences of the theory of newforms due to
Roberts and Schmidt
[
RS07
]
. (This theory assumes that we
are working with representations of trivial central character, but
this is harmless, as we can reduce to this case by twisting
\pi

by the everywhere unramified character
|\cdot|
.) Recall that for
each prime
l
and each
n\geq 0
, the paramodular group of
level
n
is

\mathrm{Par}(l^{n}):=\{g\in\GSp_{4}({\mathbf{Q}}_{l})\mid\nu(g)\in{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}^{\times}\}\cap\begin{pmatrix}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&l^{-n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}\\
l^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}\\
l^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}\\
l^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&l^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&l^{n}{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}&{\mathbf{Z}}_{l}\\
\end{pmatrix}

We say that
\pi_{l}
is
paramodular
if

(\pi_{l})^{\mathrm{Par}(l^{n})}\neq 0
for some
n
. The minimal such
n
is
the
paramodular level

N_{\pi_{l}}
of
\pi_{l}
.

The following result summarizes the facts that we need about
paramodular vectors in cuspidal automorphic representations
\pi

of
\GSp_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
.

Proposition 7.5.1
.

Suppose
that
\pi
is of general type.

(1)

For each prime
l
, there is a unique paramodular
representation in the
L
-packet containing
\pi_{l}
, namely the
unique generic representation.

(2)

If
\pi_{l}
is generic, then

(\pi_{l})^{\mathrm{Par}(l^{N_{\pi_{l}}})}
is one-dimensional.

(3)

The paramodular level
N_{\pi_{l}}
coincides
with the conductor of the corresponding
L
-parameter

{\operatorname{rec}}_{\operatorname{GT}}(\pi_{l})
.

(4)

If
\pi
is regular algebraic and
\rho_{\pi,p}
is
irreducible, then
N_{\pi_{l}}
coincides with the conductor of

\rho_{\pi,p}|_{G_{{\mathbf{Q}}_{l}}}
.

Proof.

The first part is
[
Sch18
, Thm. 1.1]
, the
second is
[
RS07
, Thm. 7.5.1]
, and the third is
[
JLRS23
, Thm. 2.3.5]
. The last claim is
[
BCGP21
, Thm. 2.7.1(2)]
(see e.g.
[
Ulm16
]
for the various
equivalent definitions of the conductor of a Galois
representation).
∎

For the following lemma we return
to the setting of Section
6.1
.

Lemma 7.5.2
.

Suppose
that
v\nmid p
and that
x_{1},x_{2}
are two closed points
of
\Spec R_{v}^{\square}[1/p]
which lie on a common irreducible
component, and are such that the corresponding lifts
\rho_{x_{1}},\rho_{x_{2}}

of
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}}
are both pure. Then we have the
equality of conductors
a(\rho_{x_{1}})=a(\rho_{x_{2}})
.

Proof.

Let
x^{\prime}_{i}
be the image of
x_{i}
in the spectrum of
the
\GL_{4}
lifting ring for
\overline{\rho}|_{G_{F^{+}_{v}}}
. These points lie on a common irreducible
component by the assumption on
x_{1},x_{2}
, and the purity
of the
\rho_{x_{i}}
ensures that this is the unique irreducible component
that either lies on (e.g. by
[
BG19
, Cor. 3.3.4]
and
the definition of purity), The result follows immediately from

[
BLGGT14
, Lem. 1.3.4(2)]
(a lemma of Choi).
∎

We now establish some instances of solvable descent for
\GSp_{4}
.

Proposition 7.5.3
.

(1)

If
\Pi
is a
|\cdot|^{2}
-self dual regular algebraic cuspidal
automorphic representation of
\GL_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
, then
(\Pi,|\cdot|^{2})
is
of symplectic type.

(2)

Let
F/{\mathbf{Q}}
be a solvable Galois extension
with
F
CM. Suppose that

\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})

has
multiplier
\varepsilon^{-1}
, that
\rho|_{G_{F}}
is
irreducible, and that there is a RACSDC automorphic
representation
\pi_{F}
of
\GL_{4}/F
such
that
\rho|_{G_{F}}\cong\rho_{\pi_{F},p}\otimes\varepsilon
.
Then there is a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic
representation
\pi
of
\GSp_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
with central
character
|\cdot|^{2}
, such that
\rho\cong\rho_{\pi,p}
.

(3)

Let
F^{+}/{\mathbf{Q}}
be a solvable Galois extension
with
F^{+}
totally real. Suppose that

\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}(\overline{{\mathbf{Q}}}_{p})
has
multiplier
\varepsilon^{-1}
, that
\rho|_{G_{F^{+}}}
is
irreducible, and that there is a regular algebraic cuspidal
automorphic representation
\pi_{F^{+}}
of
\GSp_{4}/F^{+}
,
with central character
|\cdot|^{2}
, and such
that
\rho|_{G_{F^{+}}}\cong\rho_{\pi_{F^{+}},p}
.

Then there is a regular algebraic cuspidal automorphic
representation
\pi
of
\GSp_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
with central
character
|\cdot|^{2}
, such that
\rho\cong\rho_{\pi,p}
.

Proof.

We begin with part (
1
). If the pair

(\Pi,|\cdot|^{2})
is not of symplectic type, then it is of
orthogonal type, so it descends to an automorphic
representation
\pi^{\alpha}
of some
\GSpin_{4}^{\alpha}/{\mathbf{Q}}
,
with central character
|\cdot|^{2}
. The central character
of
\pi^{\alpha}_{\infty}
can be read off from its

L
-parameter.
Under our assumption
that
\Pi
is regular algebraic (i.e.
C
-algebraic),
it follows from
[
Pat15
, Lem. 3.2(3), 3.4]
that the central character must be odd,
which means in particular that it
cannot equal
|\cdot|^{2}
.
This
contradiction implies that
(\Pi,|\cdot|^{2})
is of symplectic type,
as claimed.

By part (
1
), in each of parts (
2
)
and (
3
) it suffices to show that there is a

|\cdot|^{2}
-self dual regular algebraic cuspidal
automorphic representation
\Pi
of
\GL_{4}/{\mathbf{Q}}
with

\rho\cong\rho_{\Pi,p}
. Indeed by (
1
) such
a
\Pi
is of symplectic type, and we can take
\pi
to be a
descent of
\Pi
. Then part (
2
) is a standard
consequence of solvable descent for
\GL_{4}
, and in particular
is a special case of
[
BLGGT14
, Lem. 2.2.2]
(bearing in mind

[
BLGGT14
, Lem. 2.2.1]
, which takes care of the twist
by
\varepsilon
). Finally for part (
3
), since

\rho_{\pi_{F^{+}},p}
is irreducible, we see that
\pi_{F^{+}}
is
of general type. Its transfer
\Pi_{{F^{+}}}
is
|\cdot|^{2}
-self
dual, and the result follows from another application of
[
BLGGT14
, Lem. 2.2.2]
.
∎

We now return to our running hypotheses, so that
\rho:G_{{\mathbf{Q}}}\to\GSp_{4}({\mathcal{O}})
is a continuous representation satisfying
Hypothesis
7.1.3
,
r
is a fixed neat prime for
\overline{\rho}
, and
{\mathcal{S}}
is the deformation
problem (
7.1.5
)
with
S
as in Definition
7.4.2
. Write
\mathfrak{q}
